# CricTakkar — Project Master Brief

---

## WHO I AM

- I have zero coding knowledge. I am completely non-technical.
- I have a laptop and can give 1.5 hours every day.
- My budget is zero — every tool used must be free.
- I am based in Maharashtra, India.
- The app is for the Indian cricket audience.

---

## HOW YOU MUST TALK TO ME

- I will describe what I want in plain language. Build it directly — don't ask me to copy-paste code, and don't rewrite whole files when a smaller edit will do.
- Don't over-explain routine code changes. Just tell me briefly what you built and why, in a few plain-English lines.
- If something breaks or you hit an error, stop and explain clearly what went wrong before continuing.
- If you need a decision from me (a design choice, a missing detail, an API key), ask directly instead of guessing.
- Still build and test one feature at a time — don't move to the next feature until the current one works.
- Still deploy to Vercel and commit to GitHub after every completed feature, automatically — don't just remind me, actually do it.
- At the start of every session, briefly tell me what we completed last time and what we're doing today.
- At the end of every session, give me a short summary of what we finished.
- **PERMANENT, NON-NEGOTIABLE (added Day 24, session 6):** after making ANY change, update, or upload — editing a file, adding data, running a verification pass, adjusting a rule, committing, pushing, deploying, anything — give me a plain list in the chat of everything that was actually done. Not a vague "done!", an actual itemized list. This applies every single time, not just at the end of a session — if I ask you to do 3 things across a conversation, I want a list after each one, not just a final summary. This exists so I can actually audit what changed instead of trusting a one-line claim.

## LEGAL BOUNDARIES (IMPORTANT — NEVER CROSS THESE)

- No cash prize contests of any kind.
- No token, coin, or virtual currency system that converts to real money.
- No paid entry for a chance to win — this requires an NOGC licence under India's 2025 Online Gaming Act.
- All gameplay is completely free.
- Revenue comes only from: ads, subscriptions, affiliate links, brand sponsorships.

---

## REVENUE MODEL

- Google AdSense: ads shown between quizzes and on results screen. Never mid-question.
- Premium subscription: ₹99–199/month via Razorpay. Benefits: ad-free, exclusive quiz packs, Pro badge on profile.
- Affiliate links: Dream11, My11Circle, MPL referral links. ₹50–500 per referred signup.
- Brand sponsorships: brands pay ₹5,000–50,000 to sponsor a weekly contest. They provide the prize (merch, vouchers). Legal and safe.
- Revenue target: ₹1–2 lakh/month at 100,000 monthly active users.

- Frontend: HTML, CSS, JavaScript — Claude Code edits the project files directly (no more copy-pasting code from chat)

- Code storage: GitHub (free) — stores all our code safely, committed and pushed automatically by Claude Code

## APP DISTRIBUTION STRATEGY — PERMANENT (Added Day 36)

CricTakkar will be distributed as a **PWA (Progressive Web App) for now**, with **Google Play Store (Android) and Apple App Store (iOS) planned for later**, once there's real user traction to justify the cost and complexity. This was decided after the user asked about native apps and we worked through the tradeoffs together — see the Day 36 log entry in Current Build Status for the full discussion.

WHAT A PWA IS: the same website, made installable. Two small additions — a manifest file (name/icon/colours) and a service worker (offline caching) — let a user's browser offer "Add to Home Screen," giving CricTakkar a real home-screen icon that opens full-screen with no browser address bar. Feels like a native app, supports push notifications on modern phones, and is still exactly the same website underneath — no code duplication, no rewrite, deploys exactly the same way (push to GitHub → Vercel auto-deploys).

WHY THIS ORDER:
- PWA is free and needs almost no extra work on top of what's already built.
- Google Play ($25 one-time) and Apple App Store ($99/year, recurring) are real costs that conflict with the standing zero-budget rule until there's a specific decision to spend on it.
- The stores bring real trust/discoverability advantages for a general Indian audience that a PWA can't match on its own — worth paying for once the app has real traction, not before.

WHEN WE MOVE TO THE STORES: cheapest realistic path is wrapping the *existing* website via Capacitor or a Trusted Web Activity, with the wrapper set to load the live Vercel site (not a bundled copy) — reuses all existing code, and every future content update (quiz questions, Wordle players, On This Day events, etc.) keeps showing up in the app instantly, exactly like it does now, without needing a new store submission each time.

IMPORTANT CATCH TO REMEMBER WHEN THAT DAY COMES: Phase 4's Premium subscription plan (₹99-199/month via Razorpay) will likely need to change specifically for the native app — Google Play and Apple both require using their own in-app billing systems for digital subscriptions sold inside an app (not Razorpay directly), taking a 15-30% cut. A common workaround is having Premium purchases happen on the website rather than inside the app's own UI. Revisit this specifically when Phase 4 and the native app timelines actually meet — don't just wire up Razorpay inside a native app shell without re-checking store policy first.

STATUS: **PWA built Day 36** — manifest.json, app icons, service worker offline caching, and an install-prompt button live on all 12 pages. See Current Build Status for full detail. Play Store/App Store not started, no cost committed yet.

## PHASE 3 MULTIPLAYER ARCHITECTURE — PERMANENT (Added Day 41)

The tech stack section above says "Real-time multiplayer: Socket.io + Node.js" hosted on "Railway.app (free tier)". **That plan was written before Railway removed its genuine free tier and is no longer buildable at zero cost.** Verified Day 41: Railway now gives a one-time $5 trial credit and then $1/month of credits, while a minimal always-on service costs roughly $0.80–1.00/month — permanently scraping the ceiling with no headroom. Render's free tier does not support WebSockets at all (paid plans only), so it isn't a fallback. Vercel and Netlify can't host WebSockets either (serverless functions only).

**DECISION (user's call, Day 41): build Phase 3 on Option A — Firestore-only, no separate server. Option B (a real Socket.io server) is documented below as a deliberate future upgrade path to revisit AFTER all of Phase 3 is built, not as an abandoned idea.**

### OPTION A — FIRESTORE-ONLY MULTIPLAYER (what we are building)

Firestore's real-time listeners already push changes to every connected client within roughly 0.3 seconds. With a 15-second timer per question, that lag is imperceptible. No server to host, no monthly cost, no cold starts, and it reuses the Firebase project already in use for auth/profiles/leaderboards.

**Data model:**
```
matches/{matchId}
  roomCode         "ABC123" — 6 chars, uppercase, shareable
  hostUid          who controls question advancement
  mode             "classic" | "speed" | "elimination" | "duel"
  status           "lobby" | "playing" | "finished"
  questionIds      fixed array chosen at start so every client agrees
  currentQuestion  0-based index
  questionStartedAt  server timestamp — the sync anchor
  players          MAP of uid -> {name, joinedAt}
  leaderboard      aggregated array, written by host only
  isPublic         boolean (matchmaking vs private room)

matches/{matchId}/answers/{uid}_{questionIndex}
  uid, questionIndex, selectedOption, isCorrect, timeTaken, answeredAt
```

**Why this exact shape — the quota is the constraint.** Firebase's free Spark plan allows 50,000 document reads and 20,000 writes per day. The naive design (one document per player, everyone listening to everyone) costs ~100,000 reads for a single 100-player match and blows the daily quota in one game. The design above instead has **the host's browser act as the server**: only the host reads the answers subcollection and computes standings, then writes one aggregated `leaderboard` field; all other players listen to just the single match document. That brings a 100-player, 10-question match down to roughly **2,000 reads and 1,010 writes** — about 20 full tournaments a day within the free tier. Any future change to this data model must preserve that "one aggregated doc, not N docs" property or the free tier breaks.

**`players` as a map, not a subcollection:** makes the lobby list one document read for everyone and joining one write. Correct for private rooms (2–10 friends). Note the limit: Firestore sustains only about 1 write/second to a single document, so 100 people joining a public tournament at once would contend. Tournament-scale joins will need a different path (staggered joins or a subcollection) when Feature 34 is built.

**Timer sync:** the host writes `currentQuestion` and `questionStartedAt` together; each client starts its own 15-second countdown when that update arrives. Network jitter between clients is ~100–300ms against a 15-second window — negligible.

**KNOWN LIMITATIONS OF OPTION A — these are accepted tradeoffs, not bugs to fix:**
1. **Cheating is possible by design.** `question-bank.js` is a public file, so the correct answers are already readable by anyone who opens the browser console — that's true of the existing single-player games too. With no server, the client also reports its own `isCorrect` and `timeTaken`. Security rules make answers immutable once written and stop a player writing another player's answer, but a determined cheater can still fake a score. Acceptable for free casual play with no cash prizes (see LEGAL BOUNDARIES — there is no money at stake, which is exactly why this is tolerable).
2. ~~**If the host closes their browser, the match stalls.**~~ **FIXED Day 48 — see "HOST MIGRATION & GRACEFUL LEAVING" below.** v1 ended the match outright with a "host left" message; now another present player's browser automatically takes over via a presence heartbeat, and the match/tournament keeps running for everyone else.
3. **Scheduled tournaments have no natural human host** (Feature 39, the daily 8pm event). Likely free solution when we get there: reuse the GitHub Actions setup already built for push notifications on Day 36 — a scheduled Action can run a Node script that acts as the tournament host using the existing Firebase service-account secret. No new cost, no new platform.

### OPTION B — SOCKET.IO + NODE SERVER (documented upgrade path, revisit after Phase 3 is complete)

Do NOT treat this as dead. Revisit it once all 14 Phase 3 features are built and there are real users, and re-evaluate against actual traffic.

**What Option B genuinely fixes, in order of how much it matters:**
1. **Real anti-cheat — the strongest reason, stronger than speed.** A server can hold the correct answers and never send them to the client until after the question closes, and can time answers itself instead of trusting the client. This is the only way to make competitive/ranked multiplayer trustworthy. If CricTakkar ever runs sponsored contests with real prizes (see REVENUE MODEL — brand sponsorships providing merch/vouchers), this stops being optional, because a prize creates a real incentive to cheat.
2. **No host-dependency.** The server is always the authority, so nobody's browser closing can stall a match, and scheduled tournaments need no host workaround.
3. **True instant sync** (~50ms vs ~300ms). Only actually matters for a future real-time buzzer mode where milliseconds decide a winner — irrelevant for 15-second questions.
4. **No per-read/write quota.** Firestore's free tier caps total daily traffic; a server's cost scales with uptime, not message count. At high volume this inverts the economics in the server's favour.

**What it costs:** roughly ₹450/month ongoing for a minimal always-on host (Railway or similar), forever. This breaks the standing zero-budget rule, so it needs an explicit decision to spend — same gate as the Play Store decision in the section above.

**Migration path if we take it:** the Firestore data model above maps almost directly onto server-held room state, so the UI, game screens, and match flow built in Phase 3 would largely survive. The realistic work is replacing the Firestore listener layer with Socket.io events and moving scoring/answer-checking from the client into the server. Build the client so match state flows through one clearly-separated module rather than being scattered across screens, so this swap stays cheap later.

**Trigger conditions to actually revisit:** any one of — (a) a sponsored contest with real prizes goes live, (b) Firestore free-tier quota starts being hit on normal traffic, (c) cheating becomes a visible complaint from real users, (d) a real-time buzzer mode is wanted.

## HOST MIGRATION & GRACEFUL LEAVING — PERMANENT (Added Day 48)

Fixes the exact gap flagged in "KNOWN LIMITATIONS OF OPTION A" item 2 above: previously, if a match's or tournament's host closed their browser (or crashed), the whole thing died for every other player — a clean tab-close explicitly killed it with a "host left" message, and a crash (no clean close) just froze it silently forever with no recovery at all. The user's explicit Day 48 spec: **the match/tournament should keep going, only the person who left should be affected — and that applies whether the person leaving is the host or not.**

**PRESENCE — the shared primitive.** While a match is `'playing'`, or a tournament is `'playing'`/`'knockout'`/`'group'`, every connected player's browser writes its own `presence.{uid}` heartbeat (a Firestore `serverTimestamp()`) every 20 seconds. This is deliberately NOT running during lobby/waiting states — there's no host-driven clock running yet, so nothing to migrate, and no reason to spend writes on it. A heartbeat this infrequent stays a small fraction of a match's existing per-question write cost (a 3-minute duel adds roughly 9 extra writes per player, against the ~30-40 the host already makes running the clock) — chosen deliberately to not reopen the exact quota concern the whole Option A design exists to avoid (see the quota math above).

**`effectiveHostUid(doc, nowMs, staleMs)` — "who should be running this right now?"** A pure function (duplicated with identical logic and constants in `multiplayer.js` for matches, and shared via `tournament-logic.js` for tournaments and the scheduled bot): the recorded `hostUid` stays the effective host for as long as their own heartbeat is fresh (under 45 seconds old — roughly 2 missed beats before someone's considered gone); once they go stale (or explicitly leave, see below), the effective host becomes the lexicographically smallest uid among whichever players ARE still fresh. Every client computes this independently off the same document and reaches the same answer — there's no separate "election" write, so there's no race to coordinate. A document with no presence data at all yet (the very first moment after creation, before anyone's first heartbeat lands) defers to the recorded host unconditionally, avoiding a chicken-and-egg problem at start time.

**CLAIMING THE ROLE.** Once a client determines it should act as host, it first writes ONLY the `hostUid` field to its own uid (`claimHostIfNeeded()` / `claimTournamentHostIfNeeded()`) before doing anything else — this is a genuine, deliberate loosening of `firestore.rules`: previously only the recorded host could write anything to a match/tournament document at all; now any current player may write that one field, unconditionally as far as the rule itself is concerned (the rule can't verify staleness — that judgment lives entirely in the client's own check). Given this app already has no money at stake anywhere (LEGAL BOUNDARIES) and an already-accepted self-writable-XP trust model (Scale & Safety Audit S3), this is a small extension of an already-tolerated risk, not a new category of one. Every OTHER host-only write (phase, leaderboard, bracket, status, eliminated) is completely unchanged — still gated on actually holding `hostUid`, which the claim step is what grants.

**MATCHES specifically — timing had to become anchor-based, not chain-based.** The old design scheduled all 3 of a question's transitions (reveal → standings → next) as one client-local chain of `setTimeout`s the instant the host started the match — if that client vanished, the whole chain died with it, unrecoverable. The new design (`multiplayer.js`) writes a `phaseAt` server timestamp alongside every phase change, and schedules exactly ONE transition at a time, timed as `(phase's full duration) − (now − phaseAt)` — so ANY client that becomes the effective host mid-phase, including a freshly-migrated one, computes the correct remaining time and picks up exactly where the match actually is, rather than either restarting the clock unfairly or losing it. Each transition's own write triggers a fresh snapshot for everyone, which re-evaluates who should drive the next step — the match self-heals continuously rather than depending on one browser's timer chain surviving start to finish.

**TOURNAMENTS specifically — migration was simpler.** `hostWatchCurrentRound`/`hostWatchAllGroupMatches` were already event-driven `onSnapshot` listeners, not a client-local timer chain, so a migrated host just needs to attach the same listeners a moment later — their own pre-existing idempotency guards (`watchersForRound`, `groupWatchersAttached`) already made this safe to call on every snapshot, no new anchor-timestamp mechanism needed there.

**LEAVING GRACEFULLY — `leftPlayers`.** Clicking "Leave" (or a best-effort `beforeunload` on tab close) during an in-progress match/tournament now writes `leftPlayers.{uid}: true` and deletes that uid's `presence` entry, instead of the old "host aborts everything / non-host silently deletes themselves" behavior. A left player stays in the `players` map (so scoring/elimination bookkeeping isn't disturbed) but is excluded from `effectiveHostUid`'s candidate pool immediately, without waiting for the 45-second staleness timeout — an explicit leave is a faster, cleaner signal than a presence timeout. Last One Standing folds `leftPlayers` into `eliminated` automatically (so a departed player can't linger as the phantom "last one standing"), and the leaderboard shows a small "left" tag next to their name so it's clear their score is frozen, not stuck. Leaving a still-in-LOBBY room/tournament is unchanged from before — there's nothing running yet to hand off, so a host leaving pre-start still just ends it.

**WALKOVERS — the no-show case, added per the user's explicit Day 48 decision (wait 4 minutes, then walk it over).** Host migration alone doesn't help when a tournament bracket pairing's OWN player never shows up at all — e.g. the deterministic match-creator (see PHASE 3 MULTIPLAYER ARCHITECTURE) never opens the app, so no match document is ever even created, and the pairing just sits as "To be decided" forever, blocking the whole round from advancing even though every other pairing already finished. `computeWalkovers()` (in `tournament-logic.js`, shared by the browser and the scheduled bot) checks every still-undecided pairing in the CURRENT round once that round has been live for at least 4 minutes (`WALKOVER_WAIT_MS`): if exactly one of the two players currently has fresh presence and the other doesn't (missing, stale, or explicitly left), the present player wins by walkover (`bracket.{slot}.winnerUid` + a `walkover: true` flag, shown in the bracket UI as "walkover" instead of "vs"). If neither or both are present, nothing is decided yet — there's no fair winner to pick, or the match should just be played normally. **Scoped deliberately to round-gated stages only** — a standalone Knockout, and a League's own knockout PLAYOFFS once qualifiers are decided (status `'knockout'` shares the exact same round machinery as `'playing'`) — and explicitly NOT to a League's GROUP stage, which by design lets 20 players work through up to 9 matches each at their own pace over hours or days (see the group-stage host-watching comment in `tournament.js`); a 4-minute-since-the-stage-began clock doesn't mean the same thing there that it does for a live round everyone's expected to be actively playing right now. If group-stage no-shows ever need their own timeout, that's a separate, longer-threshold policy decision for later, not an extension of this one.

**THE SCHEDULED BOT NEEDS THIS EVEN MORE THAN A BROWSER-HOSTED TOURNAMENT DOES.** `run-scheduled-tournaments.js` has no human host to ever manually rescue a stuck bracket — so `advanceKnockoutTournament()` now also calls the exact same `computeWalkovers()` from `tournament-logic.js`, reading the REAL presence data the players' own browsers wrote while viewing `tournament.html` for that event (the bot itself has no browser tab of its own — nothing to migrate there, since a fresh cron run already re-derives everything from Firestore every 5 minutes with no client state to lose — but it can still read theirs). `roundStartedAt` is now written at every point a round begins, in both the browser code and the bot: `startKnockoutTournament()`, every `maybeAdvanceRound()`/`advanceKnockoutTournament()` round-advance, and the League→knockout-playoff transition in both `tournament.js` and the bot.

**VERIFIED:** every new pure function (`effectiveHostUid`, `isPresenceFresh`, `computeWalkovers` in both `multiplayer.js`'s and `tournament-logic.js`'s copies) tested directly in the browser against real staleness/migration/walkover scenarios — host-stays-host, host-goes-stale-and-migrates-to-lowest-fresh-uid, explicit-leave-migrates-immediately-even-if-presence-still-looks-fresh, nobody-present-returns-null, walkover-too-early-does-nothing, walkover-fires-when-exactly-one-side-present, walkover-does-nothing-when-both-or-neither-present, byes/already-decided slots never touched. `driveHostPhase`'s remaining-time math tested with a stubbed `setTimeout` across all 3 phases including a deliberately overdue case (clamps to 0, not negative) and a missing-`phaseAt` fallback. `maybeActAsHost`/`maybeActAsTournamentHost` tested end-to-end against a stubbed Firestore: becomes driving host, claims an abandoned `hostUid`, is a true no-op on an unchanged snapshot, and correctly stands down the moment it's no longer the effective host. `maybeApplyWalkovers` tested through a full round-completion cycle (walkover + a normally-decided pairing together completing the round, seeding the next round's pairing, writing a fresh `roundStartedAt`). Leaderboard's "left" tag and its XSS-safety were checked directly in the DOM. **NOT yet verified: any of this with a real crash/disconnect across two or more real accounts** — same category of gap as every other Phase 3 feature before its first live multi-account test; the underlying mechanism (a real match/tournament, real presence writes, real host-claim writes) needs the updated `firestore.rules` published in the Firebase console first (same standing lesson as every previous rules change in this project — committing the file changes nothing on its own), then a real test where one browser tab is deliberately closed mid-match/mid-tournament to confirm another one actually picks it up.

## HOST-LESS SCHEDULED TOURNAMENTS — PERMANENT (Added Day 47)

Features #39 (Daily 8pm scheduled tournament) and #40 (IPL season special tournaments) both needed a tournament with no human host — every other Phase 3 tournament (manual Knockout, manual League + Playoffs) is driven by whichever player's browser tab created it, which doesn't work for a standing daily event nobody personally owns. Built as **two tournaments a day, always both**: an 8:00 PM IST Knockout and a 10:00 PM IST League + Playoffs, per the user's explicit Day 47 decision (not "whichever gets enough signups" — both run every night, independently).

**ARCHITECTURE — a GitHub Actions cron bot plays the host role instead of a browser tab.** Reuses the exact same service-account pattern as the Day 36 push-notification sender (`FIREBASE_SERVICE_ACCOUNT` secret, Firebase Admin SDK, which bypasses Firestore security rules entirely). The one real architectural difference from every human-hosted tournament: **a one-shot cron invocation can't hold a persistent `onSnapshot` listener open** the way a browser tab can, so the bot polls instead — every run does a plain `.get()` on whatever's unresolved and writes what it finds, rather than reacting to a live push. `.github/workflows/run-scheduled-tournaments.yml` runs `automation/run-scheduled-tournaments.js` every 5 minutes across the evening (UTC 14:00–19:55 = IST 19:30–01:25 next day) — comfortably covers both lock times plus several hours of match-completion polling afterward. **Accepted limitation, same shape as the human-host tradeoff already documented above:** if a tournament is somehow still unfinished after that window closes, it just resumes advancing at the next evening's first run — nothing is lost, the tournament doc just sits idle until then. **Day 48 addition:** within that window, a pairing whose player(s) never show up at all no longer stalls the whole round forever either — see "HOST MIGRATION & GRACEFUL LEAVING" above, whose walkover mechanism this bot now also runs.

**SHARED LOGIC, NOT A SECOND COPY.** The bracket/round-robin/seeding math (`buildInitialBracket`, `nextRoundPairings`, `isRoundComplete`, `generateRoundRobinSchedule`, `computeGroupStandings`, `seedKnockoutBracket`, etc.) used to live only inside `tournament.js`, which is browser-only code tightly coupled to the DOM and the Firebase compat SDK. On Day 47 these pure, dependency-free functions were extracted into `tournament-logic.js` at the repo root, written to load two different ways — `<script>` tag in the browser (attaches everything to `window` as globals, exactly as before) or `require()` in Node (returns the same functions via `module.exports`) — so the bot and the browser UI share one source of truth instead of a hand-copied second version that could quietly drift out of sync. `tournament.js` itself lost nothing functionally; every call site was updated to the (mostly identical) shared signatures and the manual Knockout/League flow was regression-tested to confirm byte-for-byte identical behavior.

**SIGN-UP IS A SEPARATE, OPEN COLLECTION — `scheduledSignups/{eventId}`** (`eventId` = `"{YYYY-MM-DD}-{knockout|league}"`, IST calendar date). This is deliberately NOT the same `/tournaments` collection the manual flow uses: sign-up needs to accept players all day with no cap, while `/tournaments` docs assume a bracket size that's already fixed at creation. Any signed-in player can create today's sign-up sheet (first one there, mirroring the existing `createTournament()` collision-check pattern) and may only add or remove their own entry from the `players` map while `status == 'open'` — every other transition (locking, cancelling, writing the real `tournamentCode`) is bot-only, enforced simply by nobody else holding the Admin SDK credential, not by a Firestore rule. **✅ Published in the Firebase console (Day 47) — these rules are live.**

**BYES — the mechanism that makes "no minimum fuss, just run it" possible.** Per the user's explicit Day 47 decision, an uneven signup count doesn't shrink the bracket or turn anyone away — it uses real tournament bye logic instead:
- **Knockout:** no cap on signups at all. At lock time, `buildInitialBracketWithByes()` rounds the count up to the next power of 2 and randomly gives that many players a free pass into round 2 — a bye is written as an *already-resolved* round-0 pairing (`player2Uid: 'BYE'`, `winnerUid` pre-set to the bye player) the instant the bracket is created, so every existing downstream function (`isRoundComplete`, `nextRoundPairings`, the bot's own round-advancement) treats it exactly like an already-played match with zero special-casing needed anywhere else — only the bracket *rendering* needed a small addition (`buildBracketHTML` shows "bye — advances automatically" instead of "vs BYE"). Minimum to run at all: **4 signups**.
- **League:** no fixed 20/10-10 split anymore either — `splitIntoTwoGroups()` divides however many signed up as evenly as possible (the odd one out goes to Group A). Since the playoff stage always takes exactly the top 4 from each group regardless of group size, the 8-player knockout that follows never needs a bye itself. Round-robin scheduling within a group also now handles an *odd* group size (`generateRoundRobinSchedule()` adds a 'BYE' seat — whoever's paired against it just sits out that one round, no match) — for an even count (including the original fixed groups of 10) this is byte-for-byte identical to the pre-Day-47 behavior. Minimum to run at all: **10 signups**.

**NOTIFICATIONS — 5 new types, all in `send-notifications.js`/`send-notifications.yml` except one:**
- **`tournament-signup`** (9:00 AM, 3:00 PM, and 6:00 PM IST — same message, 3 separate daily reminders) — "tonight's tournaments are open," to everyone opted in.
- **`knockout-starting-soon`** (7:45 PM IST, 15 minutes before the 8pm lock) and **`league-starting-soon`** (9:45 PM IST, 15 minutes before the 10pm lock) — a last chance to sign up before it's too late. Per the user's explicit Day 47 call, these go to **everyone opted in, not just people who've already joined** — the point is pulling in people who haven't signed up yet, not just reminding people who are already in.
- All 4 of the above reuse the already-tested `fetchOptedInUsers()`/`sendToUsers()` machinery already in `send-notifications.js` — same broad-audience pattern as Streak/On This Day/etc.
- **Cancellation is the one exception, and stays out of that file on purpose.** Only the scheduled-tournament bot knows, at the exact moment it decides not to run tonight's event, that it's cancelling — so that notice is sent directly from `run-scheduled-tournaments.js` itself, to just the (small) list of players who'd actually signed up for that specific event, not the whole opted-in user base. This is a deliberately different targeting shape from every other tournament notification above.

**VERIFIED SO FAR:** every piece of new logic (bye-bracket construction, uneven group-splitting, odd-count round-robin, the bot's lock/advance/cancel/notify functions) was tested against real inputs — the pure math directly in the browser (Node isn't available in this environment's shell), and the bot's actual functions by extracting them from the real file and running them against a mocked in-memory Firestore, not a hand-written reimplementation. An 11-signup Knockout correctly rounds up to a 16-slot bracket with 5 byes and plays through cleanly to a champion; a 13-signup League splits 7/6, completes its full round-robin, and transitions into an 8-player knockout exactly like the manual flow's transition does; an under-minimum event cancels and notifies the right (and only the right) opted-in signups. **NOT yet verified: a real GitHub Actions run, and a real signup from an actual account.** Same category of gap as every other Phase 3 feature before its first live test — this one additionally needs the rules republished (see above) before any of it will work for a real user at all.

## SCALE & SAFETY AUDIT — PARKED FOR REVIEW (Added Day 42)

**STATUS: FINDINGS ONLY. NOTHING HERE HAS BEEN FIXED.** The user asked on Day 42 whether the current build is the right way to reach 1 million players, and asked for the answer to be recorded rather than acted on immediately. This section is the record. Come back to it as a dedicated review session — do NOT quietly fix items from here mid-way through unrelated feature work, because several fixes change data shape and need the user's decision first.

**THE VERDICT, SO A FUTURE SESSION DOESN'T RE-LITIGATE IT:** the architecture is sound and must NOT be rebuilt. Static files on a CDN is the most scalable frontend design available — there is no server to fall over, and Vercel serves 1 user or 1 million identically. No framework means no build step and no dependency rot, which is a genuine strength for a solo non-technical builder, not a compromise. Every problem below is a *data-access pattern*, not a frontend-technology problem. Rewriting in React/Next.js would cost weeks and fix none of them.

**WHY IT'S SAFE TO DEFER (mostly):** every item below is invisible and harmless at current traffic. They are all "scale cliffs" — fine at hundreds of users, catastrophic at hundreds of thousands — except items S1 and S2, which are live risks at ANY user count and should not wait for the big review.

### CATEGORY A — LIVE SAFETY ISSUES (do NOT defer these two)

**S1. ✅ CODE FIXED Day 44 — NOT YET FULLY IN FORCE, see below.** Every user's email address was readable by any signed-in user — the most serious finding in the audit. `login.html:164` and `login.html:309` wrote `email` into the user's Firestore document, and `firestore.rules:35` set `allow read: if signedIn()` on `/users/{userId}`. Combined, that meant anyone who created a free account could read the email address of every registered user — plus their FCM push token and real name. At 1,000 users that's a bad look. At 1,000,000 it is a one-million-record personal-data breach available to anyone with a browser, and India's Digital Personal Data Protection Act 2023 applies to it. Fixed by splitting the user document: public ranking fields (username, xp, level, favouriteTeam, badges) stay on `/users/{uid}`, while `email`, `fcmToken`, and real `name` now live in `/users/{uid}/private/data`, a subcollection only that uid can read (new rule in `firestore.rules`). `login.html`'s two signup paths, `profile.html`'s blank-doc-creation path, and `profile.html`'s `saveSetup()` all write to the split location now; `notifications.js`'s `enableNotifications()` writes `fcmToken` there too. Existing accounts self-migrate the next time they open `profile.html`: `migrateLegacyPrivateFields()` copies any leftover `name`/`email`/`fcmToken` off the public doc into the private one, then deletes them from the public doc — copy-then-delete, so a failed write (rules not yet published) never loses data, just retries next visit. The GitHub Actions notification sender (`automation/send-notifications.js`) was updated to read `fcmToken` from the private subcollection (one extra read per opted-in user per run via the Admin SDK, which bypasses rules anyway — negligible at current scale). **Two things still outstanding, both needed before this is actually protecting anyone:** (1) **the updated `firestore.rules` needs to be published in the Firebase console again** — exactly the S2 lesson from Day 42, committing the file changes nothing on its own, and until it's published the self-migration will keep failing safely (harmlessly retrying, not exposing anything worse, but also not fixing anything) and new signups' private-doc writes will fail outright; (2) **no real account has been tested end-to-end** — everything was verified with mocked Firestore calls (3 migration scenarios: successful migration, blocked-by-unpublished-rules, and already-migrated — all behaved correctly), since creating real accounts is outside what an automated session can do. After publishing the rules, do one real signup and one real login-as-an-existing-account to confirm the private doc actually gets created/populated and the public doc actually loses the fields.

**S2. ✅ RESOLVED Day 42 (commit `324f353`).** `firestore.rules` was sitting untracked on disk. It is now committed alongside the Phase 3 multiplayer build. The design notes from the audit still stand and are worth keeping: it correctly makes multiplayer answers immutable, pins answer document IDs to `{uid}_{questionIndex}` so a player cannot write duplicate answers to inflate a score, stops non-hosts rewriting the leaderboard, and blocks players reading each other's answers before the reveal. **Still outstanding on this item: the file has NOT been deployed to the Firebase console yet**, so none of it is actually in force — committing a rules file changes nothing until it is published. See the Day 42 log entry below for the deploy steps the user still needs to run, and read S1 first, because deploying this file as written locks in the email-exposure behaviour described there.

**S3. XP is self-writable, so the leaderboard is cheatable.** `firestore.rules:36` correctly stops you writing to *someone else's* profile, but nothing stops you writing `xp: 999999999` to your *own*. Any player who opens the browser console can top the global leaderboard. This is an accepted, documented consequence of the no-server design (same root cause as the multiplayer cheating limitation already noted in the PHASE 3 section above) and it is tolerable precisely because LEGAL BOUNDARIES forbid cash prizes — there is nothing to win. **It stops being tolerable the moment a sponsored contest with a real prize goes live (Feature 46).** Treat that as a hard gate: no prize contest ships while XP is client-written.

### CATEGORY B — SCALE CLIFFS (safe to defer, but they get more expensive to fix the longer they wait)

**S4. The Percentile Rank card reads the ENTIRE users collection on every view.** `stats.html:825` runs `db.collection('users').orderBy('xp','desc').get()` with no limit, downloading every user document to compute one person's percentile. Cost scales linearly and brutally: 1,000 users = 1,000 reads per page view (free); 50,000 users = one page view consumes the entire daily free-tier quota; 1,000,000 users = roughly ₹50 per single page view, so 1,000 views in a day would cost about ₹50,000. **This is the single worst line of code in the project for scale.** Fix: maintain one small document holding an XP distribution histogram (how many players fall in each XP band), updated periodically, so percentile costs 1 read instead of 1,000,000. Roughly a 30-minute fix. `stats.html:883` has a similar but far milder pattern for the friends comparison — that one is correctly chunked and bounded by friend count, so it is fine.

**S5. The "weekly" and "monthly" leaderboard tabs are actually all-time.** `leaderboard.html:493` runs the same `orderBy('xp','desc').limit(50)` query regardless of which tab is selected — there is no time filter anywhere. The query itself is efficient and scales fine; the problem is a product one. At scale the top 50 becomes permanently frozen, no new player can ever appear on it, and the competitive loop that drives retention dies. Fix needs `weeklyXp` / `monthlyXp` fields on the user document plus a reset mechanism — the existing GitHub Actions cron (already built for push notifications on Day 36) can do the reset for free, no new platform needed.

**S6. The content data files ship whole to every visitor and grow every single day.** `players.js` is 202 KB, `question-bank.js` is 181 KB, `onthisday.js` is 78 KB. `quiz.html:143` loads the entire 181 KB question bank just to ask 10 questions. Gzip cuts this roughly 3-4x so it is survivable today, and CDN caching means repeat visits are cheap — but the automation adds to these files daily and forever, and the target audience is mid-range Android phones on Indian mobile data. This is the slow rot: not urgent, but it should be fixed before the files double again. Fix is mechanical because `question-bank.js` is already category-tagged: split per category, load only what a page needs.

### CATEGORY C — COMMERCIAL / PLATFORM GATES

**S7. Vercel's free plan forbids commercial use.** The moment Phase 4 puts AdSense on the site, a paid Vercel plan is required or the terms are being violated. Check this before ads go live, not after.

**S8. Firebase's free tier runs out at roughly 2,000–4,000 daily active players** (Spark: 50,000 reads/day, 20,000 writes/day). This is not a design flaw, just where free ends. Rough cost at 1M monthly players is ₹40,000–1,50,000/month, depending heavily on whether S4 is fixed first. The revenue target (₹1–2 lakh/month at 100k users) covers it, but only with sane read patterns — which makes S4 a revenue issue, not just a technical one.

**RECOMMENDED ORDER WHEN THIS REVIEW HAPPENS:** S2 (commit rules, trivial) → S1 (email exposure, the real one) → S4 (the cost bomb) → S5 (leaderboard) → S6 (file splitting). S3 stays open by design until a prize contest is on the table. S7/S8 are checks, not builds.

## BUILD RULES — FOLLOW EVERY SINGLE SESSION

1. Start every session by telling me what we completed last time and what today's goal is.
2. End every session by giving me a short summary of what we finished today.
3. Build and test one feature completely before touching the next.
4. Commit to GitHub and deploy to Vercel automatically after every completed feature — don't just remind me, actually do it.
5. If something breaks or errors out, stop and fix it immediately — do not continue building until it's resolved.
6. Edit project files directly. Use small, targeted edits rather than rewriting whole files unless a full rewrite is genuinely needed.
7. Launch after Phase 1 Day 12. Do not wait for the full app to be ready.
8. If I ask what day of building we are on, calculate it from Phase 1 Day 1 onwards.
9. When showing me a built feature, always show it as a Claude Artifact (a real preview I can open and click through, using sample data if needed) — never just a live browser preview or a description. Claude can still use a live browser internally to test and verify the code works, but what gets shown to me must be an Artifact.

## MODEL SELECTION RULE — PERMANENT (Added Day 12)

I cannot judge on my own which Claude model (Sonnet vs Opus vs Haiku) is right for a given piece of work, and Claude cannot switch the main conversation model itself — only I can do that, in the app's model picker. So Claude must proactively tell me which model to be on.

DEFAULT: Sonnet 5 for almost everything in this project — it's a mostly straightforward frontend/Firebase app and Sonnet is the right cost/quality balance.

WHEN TO SWITCH TO OPUS:
- Phase 3 (Multiplayer) — designing the real-time Socket.io architecture, tournament sync, and matchmaking logic. Once that architecture is settled, routine feature work in Phase 3 can drop back to Sonnet.
- Phase 4 (Money Features) — Razorpay payment integration and admin dashboard access control. Money handling and access control deserve the extra care.
- Any bug Claude is still stuck on after 2+ attempts on Sonnet.
- A contested cricket fact (record holder, "most/fastest/highest") that stays unclear even after a web search under the Question Quality Rule.

NEVER use Haiku to build this project — quality and reliability matter more than raw speed given the legal and financial stakes (Online Gaming Act, Razorpay payments).

WHAT CLAUDE MUST DO:
- At the start of every new feature or phase, state plainly which model I should be on, e.g. "Use Sonnet 5 for this" or "Switch to Opus for this one — here's why."
- Do this even when the answer is "stay on Sonnet" — don't assume I'll remember or guess correctly.

## QUESTION QUALITY RULE — PERMANENT (Added after Day 2)

Every question added to CricTakkar — by me or by Claude — must pass this checklist before going into the app.

CHECKLIST (all boxes must be true):
- Fact passes the FOUR-SOURCE VERIFICATION RULE below (Cricbuzz + ESPNcricinfo + Wikipedia + ICC official records, all matching)
- All 4 options are plausible (not obviously silly wrong answers)
- The fun fact adds something new — not just repeating the answer
- No "as of today" answers that will become outdated (e.g. "current world record holder")
- **No hints embedded in the question wording (added Day 26, session 7):** the question text must test the fact cleanly, with no extra clause that tips off or telegraphs the answer (e.g. "...a feat matched only once before by X" inside the question itself). Any such context — the comparison, the rarity, the "why this matters" detail — belongs in the fun fact shown after the answer is revealed, not before. If a draft question reads like it's giving away its own answer, move that context into the fact field and reword the question to ask cleanly. This applies to every question added from now on, by Claude or by the automation, in every category.

QUESTIONS WE WILL NEVER WRITE:
- Anything involving "hat-trick" unless verified on ESPNcricinfo first
- Any "fastest", "most", "highest" record without checking the exact current record holder
- Any question about a match result from memory
- Any statistic with a specific number without verifying the source

WHAT CLAUDE MUST DO:
- Every time Claude writes a new question, Claude must state clearly: "Verified on ESPNcricinfo — [what the source confirms]"
- If Claude is not 100% certain, Claude must say "I am not fully certain — please verify this on ESPNcricinfo before adding"
- Never add a question just because it sounds right
- Before adding any question involving "most titles", "current champion", "who holds the record" or any fact that changes over time — do a web search first to confirm the latest answer. Do not rely on training data for live records.

## FOUR-SOURCE VERIFICATION RULE — PERMANENT (Added Day 16)

This is the master fact-checking standard for the entire app. It applies to every question, fact, and data point across ALL content areas: Daily Quiz + Category Quiz — IPL/Test/ODI/T20 (all now live in one central, category-tagged file, `question-bank.js`, as of Day 17), On This Day (`onthisday.js`), Ranking Challenge (`ranking.js`), Cricket Poetry — poems and Poetry Quiz (`poems.js`), and Cricket Wordle (`players.js`). It supersedes the older 2-source ("ESPNcricinfo + Wikipedia") wording used elsewhere in this file — wherever this file says to check 2 sources, this 4-source rule now takes precedence.

STANDING SCOPE — THIS RULE IS NOT LIMITED TO THE LIST ABOVE: it automatically applies to any new quiz mode, category, sub-category, game, or content area added to CricTakkar at any point in the future (e.g. a future T20 World Cup category, a new Phase 2/3 feature with cricket facts, a new player database for another game mode) — Claude does not need a separate instruction to apply it each time. It also applies to any additional data the user or Claude adds to an existing area later — more questions added to any quiz, more players added to Wordle, more events added to On This Day, more challenges added to Ranking, more poems added to Poetry. The rule runs by default on all cricket facts/data in this codebase, existing or future, unless the user explicitly says otherwise for a specific case.

THE RULE:
- Every fact must be checked against all 4 of: **the Cricbuzz website (cricbuzz.com), ESPNcricinfo, Wikipedia, and ICC official records** (icc-cricket.com). (Wording clarified Day 28: "Cricbuzz" throughout this file specifically means the cricbuzz.com website — see the KNOWN TOOL LIMITATION note below on why it's flagged unreachable every time.)
- MECHANISM — NOT OPTIONAL (added Day 17, session 6, after a real lapse): "checked against" means an explicit, separate, domain-restricted search per site — using WebSearch's `allowed_domains` parameter set to exactly one of espncricinfo.com / wikipedia.org / icc-cricket.com / cricbuzz.com per query — for every single fact. A broad, unrestricted web search that happens to cite some of these sites in its results is NOT the same thing and does NOT satisfy this rule, even if the fact looks consistent across what came back. The lapse that triggered this clause: an entire batch of 30 questions (Day 17, session 5) was "verified" using broad searches instead of per-site ones — the facts turned out fine on a spot re-check, but the process itself didn't match what this rule requires, and the user caught it, not Claude. Default to the strictest reading of this rule, always — it is described as non-negotiable and hardcore by the user, so ambiguity gets resolved toward more rigor, never toward what's faster.
- This is an AND, not an OR. All four sources must show the exact same fact/number/detail.
- Zero tolerance: ANY mismatch between the sources, however small (e.g. 703 vs 704 wickets, "left-arm orthodox" vs "left-arm unorthodox", one team missing from an IPL list), counts as a mismatch.
- Claude never resolves a mismatch on its own — no picking the "more likely" source, no majority rule, no judgment call. Every mismatch gets flagged to the user instead.
- Flags are collected together and presented as one list at the end of a batch/file, not one at a time as they come up.
- The user decides the resolution for each flagged item (fix it a specific way, drop it, or keep with a caveat comment). Claude waits for that decision before writing the data.
- Applies to BOTH the existing database (a full re-verification pass, started Day 16, working through content areas one at a time) and every new question/fact/player added from now on — nothing new goes in without clearing all 4 sources first.

KNOWN TOOL LIMITATION (found Day 16): Claude's web tools (WebSearch/WebFetch) cannot access **the Cricbuzz website, cricbuzz.com specifically** — it blocks the crawler on both domain-filtered search and direct fetch. (This is specifically about the cricbuzz.com website being blocked to Claude's tools — not a statement about Cricbuzz as a brand/source being unreliable.) Per user decision: Cricbuzz stays in the rule, and every single time it is unreachable, Claude must explicitly flag that fact as "Cricbuzz website unreachable" alongside the verification result — this is a standing, permanent flag, not a one-time note. Verification proceeds on the other 3 sources (ESPNcricinfo + Wikipedia + ICC) when the Cricbuzz website can't be reached, but the gap must always be visible to the user, never silently skipped.

KNOWN TOOL LIMITATION, PART 2 (found Day 18, IPL Batch 2): direct WebFetch of iplt20.com pages (stats pages, points tables) returns 403 Forbidden, same failure mode as Cricbuzz — and the same is true of direct WebFetch on espncricinfo.com stat pages specifically (search-based access to espncricinfo.com still works fine). WebSearch with `allowed_domains` set to iplt20.com DOES still return usable results, but only when the query is phrased with the specific expected fact/number already in it — vague queries return only page titles/links with no real content in the AI-synthesized summary. So for IPL facts: run the targeted iplt20.com WebSearch query, and if it doesn't confirm the number, don't treat that as a real source mismatch — retry with a more specific query before concluding anything. Separately, and unrelated to reachability: WebSearch's own AI-synthesized answer text has repeatedly proven unreliable on exact numbers even when the underlying source page is reachable (e.g. it misquoted Bhuvneshwar Kumar's IPL 2017 wicket count as 23 instead of 26, apparently cross-contaminating it with his 2016 tally, during Batch 2 verification) — always cross-check a synthesized number against a second, independent query before trusting it.

SCOPE AT TIME OF WRITING (Day 16): ~155 existing items across Daily Quiz, Category Quiz (4 categories), On This Day, and Ranking Challenge, plus 130 Wordle players × 8 attributes each, all need to be re-verified under this standard. See Current Build Status for progress through the backlog.

## ADDING NEW CONTENT — STANDARD OPERATING PROCEDURE (Added Day 17, session 5)

This is the permanent, repeatable process Claude follows any time the user says "add questions" / "add players" / "add [X]" to any content area — the user should never need to re-explain this process. It applies to all 5 content files: `question-bank.js` (quiz questions), `players.js` (Wordle), `onthisday.js` (dated events), `ranking.js` (challenges), `poems.js` (poems + Poetry Quiz).

**Step 1 — Figure out the target file and shape.** Match the request to the right file (see the field shapes documented earlier in this file for each). If the user gives a topic/player/theme rather than full content, Claude drafts the actual question/entry itself.

**Step 2 — Check for duplicates.** Before writing anything, check the existing bank for the same fact already covered under different phrasing (this is what caused the Day 17 merge cleanup). Skip or merge instead of adding a near-duplicate.

**Step 3 — Verify every fact.** Run the Four-Source Verification Rule above on every new fact — live web search, not training data. Cricbuzz is expected to be unreachable (standing tool limitation) — flag it every time, don't silently skip it. If a fact is a "current record" / "most X" / "reigning champion" type that can change over time (Question Quality Rule), handle it explicitly: note in the fact text if the record is still growing (an active player), confirm if the record is now closed (a retired player), and phrase dated tournament results around their specific year rather than "current champion" so the question never goes stale.

**Step 4 — Tag correctly.** For `question-bank.js`: assign `category` (ipl/test/odi/t20/general) and `era` (Pre-1970s/1970s-80s/1990s/2000s/2010s/2020s/General) per the existing tagging rules. For other files, follow that file's existing field schema exactly (e.g. Wordle's 8 attributes, Ranking's 5-item ordered list).

**Step 5 — Structural validation.** Before considering the content done, run a quick integrity check confirming every new entry has all required fields, exactly 4 options where applicable, and a valid correct-answer index — this catches typos/missing fields before they ever reach a user.

**Step 6 — Test live in the browser.** Load the relevant page(s), confirm the new content renders correctly with no console errors, and confirm any live counts (home page stat strip, category pool sizes) updated correctly with zero manual editing needed elsewhere.

**Step 7 — Report back plainly.** Give the user the full list of what was added (not just a count), any facts that needed special "growing record" handling, and explicitly confirm the verification pass ran (which sources, Cricbuzz status) rather than just asserting "verified" — this is what lets the user actually check the work, not just trust it blindly.

**Step 8 — Commit, push, deploy, and update Current Build Status** — per the standing project rule, automatically, without being asked.

If at any point a fact can't be confirmed clean across the reachable sources, it gets flagged to the user per the Four-Source Verification Rule's mismatch-handling process — Claude never guesses or picks a "likely" answer to keep moving.

## QUIZ QUESTION EXPANSION PROJECT — PERMANENT (Added Day 26, session 8)

Started because the original curated curricula for `question-bank.js` (IPL season history, ODI World Cup history, T20 World Cup history, Test iconic moments, General terminology) are all complete — this defines how new questions keep getting added indefinitely from here on, by Claude or by the automation, without needing a new hand-picked project every time.

**CATEGORY ROTATION — fixed sequence, not self-balancing:** `general` → `ipl` → `test` → `odi` → `t20` → back to `general`. One category per run/session, always the next one in this exact order regardless of current pool sizes. (Counts as of Day 26 session 8: ipl 93, odi 88, test 54, t20 72, general 23 — general is smallest today, but that's not why it's first; it's just next in the fixed sequence after t20 wrapped around.)

**SOURCING METHOD — open research, not a fixed sub-curriculum:** for whichever category is up, search broadly for well-documented, verifiable cricket facts/records/milestones/moments in that category that aren't already covered in `question-bank.js` (check Step 2 of the Adding New Content SOP — read the existing questions in that category first so nothing gets duplicated under different phrasing). There's no preset list of sub-topics to work through; each run finds its own genuinely notable, checkable facts.

**EVERY NEW QUESTION MUST ALSO PASS:**
- The Four-Source Verification Rule (ESPNcricinfo + Wikipedia + ICC + Cricbuzz-attempted-unreachable, all agree)
- The Question Quality Rule checklist, including the no-embedded-hints rule (Day 26 session 7) — the question wording tests the fact cleanly with no clause that tips off the answer; any rarity/comparison/context goes in the fun fact instead
- Correct `category` and `era` tagging per the existing rules

**BATCH SIZE — NO TARGET, WORK TO THE ACTUAL LIMIT (hardened Day 28):** there is no "normal" number of questions per run. An earlier version of this rule mentioned "8-12 per run" as a baseline — that number must never be treated as a target, a default, or something to stop near. Every run (manual or automated) keeps researching and verifying NEW, non-duplicate, well-documented questions in the assigned category for as long as real usable capacity remains in that session — stopping ONLY when a genuine hard limit is hit (context/tokens/turns actually running out) or there is truly no more verifiable material left to find in that category, never because a round number was reached. If a batch happens to land anywhere near 8-12, or 30, or 3, that's incidental to how much capacity and material existed that session — not evidence the run did enough.

**TRACKING:** the `AUTOMATION LAST QUESTION CATEGORY:` line in Current Build Status records which category was done last, so the next "questions" run advances to the next one in the fixed sequence. Create this line (defaulting to starting the sequence at `general`) if it doesn't exist yet.

## CRICKET WORDLE PLAYER DATA RULE — PERMANENT (Added Day 10, updated Day 14)

The Cricket Wordle game uses a player database in `players.js`. Every player entry has 8 guessable attributes: country, role, battingStyle, bowlingStyle, debutYear, format, iplTeams, iccTrophies.

- `bowlingStyle` — added Day 14. Mark "NA" only if the player has a genuinely confirmed zero-ball bowling record (e.g. Rishabh Pant, Sanju Samson — explicitly 0 overs/wickets in their career stats). If they have bowled even a single delivery anywhere in their career — even just once, even a part-timer's over — record their actual bowling style instead of NA. Caught this the hard way on Day 14: MS Dhoni was wrongly marked NA despite having bowled in internationals (took 1 wicket, 2009 Champions Trophy, right-arm medium). If no source can confirm bowling record either way (bowled or never bowled), mark NA but add a comment flagging it as unconfirmed rather than proven, so it can be revisited (see KL Rahul's entry as the template for this).
- `bowlingStyle` NAMING STANDARD — PERMANENT (added Day 33, after a full-database audit found 23 different spellings of what were really only 13 distinct bowling types — e.g. "Right-arm legbreak", "Legbreak", "Right-arm leg break", "Right-arm legbreak googly" were all being used for the same thing, causing `comparePlayer()`'s exact-string-match logic to wrongly show grey instead of green for players who actually share a bowling style). Every `bowlingStyle` value — new or edited — MUST be exactly one of these 13 strings, written exactly as shown (space-separated, no hyphens, no commas):
  - `Right arm offbreak`
  - `Right arm medium`
  - `Right arm fast`
  - `Right arm fast medium`
  - `Right arm medium fast` (kept distinct from "fast medium" — a deliberate real pace-category distinction, not a duplicate)
  - `Right arm leg break` (also covers what other sources may call "leg spin," "legbreak googly," or "leg break googly" — the googly-variation distinction is not tracked separately in this app)
  - `Left arm orthodox` (also covers "slow left-arm orthodox" — same thing)
  - `Left arm fast`
  - `Left arm fast medium`
  - `Left arm medium fast` (kept distinct from "fast medium," same reasoning as the right-arm pair)
  - `Left arm medium`
  - `Left Arm Wrist Spin (Chinaman)` (also covers plain "left-arm wrist-spin" without the chinaman label — same thing; this is the one deliberate exception to the no-punctuation rule, since the parenthetical is part of the agreed name, not a hyphen/comma)
  - `NA`
  No other spelling, abbreviation, hyphenation, or word order may be used for any of these — if a source describes a player's bowling in different words (e.g. "leg-spin", "chinaman bowler", "medium pace"), map it to the correct one of these 13 exact strings before writing it into `players.js`. If a genuinely new bowling type that doesn't fit any of these 13 comes up (e.g. a specialist type not yet seen in the database), flag it to the user before inventing a new spelling convention on the fly. `wordle.js`'s `bowlingStyleAbbr` map (used for the compact clue-table display) must be kept in sync with this exact list of keys.
- `iplTeams` — added Day 14, replaces the old single `iplTeam` + `iplTeamsCount` fields. An array of every IPL team the player has actually played a match for, in chronological order — not just their current or most recent team. A team they were signed to but never played a game for (e.g. Axar Patel's Mumbai Indians stint) does not count. Empty array `[]` for players who never played IPL.
- IPL TEAM LIST SEARCH METHOD — PERMANENT (added Day 14, after Batch 2 needed a second correction pass): a single combined search query (e.g. "Player X batting bowling style IPL teams") reliably surfaces batting/bowling style but is NOT reliable for the full IPL team list — it tends to surface only the player's most recent or most prominent team and silently miss earlier ones. This caused 6 real misses in Batch 2 alone (Guptill, Ponting, Gilchrist, Russell, Chanderpaul, Ross Taylor), including two players wrongly marked "Didn't play IPL" who had actually played for multiple franchises. Always run a SEPARATE, dedicated query for IPL history specifically — phrase it as "complete IPL career all teams" or "did X ever play IPL" rather than folding it into the combined batting/bowling query — for every player, not just the ones that seem like they might have a complicated history. This is now mandatory before marking any player's `iplTeams` as a single team or empty array.
- CROSS-VERIFICATION STANDARD: every one of the 8 attributes (not just batting/bowling style and IPL teams) must pass the FOUR-SOURCE VERIFICATION RULE above — Cricbuzz + ESPNcricinfo + Wikipedia + ICC official records, zero-tolerance AND logic. Any mismatch is flagged to the user, collected together per batch, never resolved silently.
- `iccTrophies` SEARCH METHOD — PERMANENT (added Day 32, after a real error was user-caught): a player's `iccTrophies` count can NEVER be set to 0, or left at whatever an earlier batch recorded, just because they don't look like a "trophy era" star or their career doesn't obviously line up with a famous win. The mistake this caught: Sunil Gavaskar (India, debut 1971, retired 1987) was marked `iccTrophies: 0` despite being an actual squad member who batted in the final of India's 1983 World Cup win — the same tournament Kapil Dev's entry (right above his in the file) correctly credits with 1. The fix: for every player, every batch, run a dedicated query against the ACTUAL OFFICIAL SQUAD LIST (not a general search, not assumed from era/fame) for every ICC trophy their country won during a plausible span of that player's career — ODI World Cup (1975/79 WI, 1983 India, 1987 Aus, 1992 Pak, 1996 SL, 1999/2003/2007 Aus, 2011 India, 2015 Aus, 2019 Eng, 2023 Aus), T20 World Cup (2007 India, 2009 Pak, 2010 Eng, 2012/2016 WI, 2014 SL, 2021 Aus, 2022 Eng, 2024/2026 India), Champions Trophy/ICC KnockOut Trophy (1998 SA, 2000 NZ, 2002 India+SL joint, 2004 WI, 2006/2009 Aus, 2013 India, 2017 Pak, 2025 India), and WTC (2021 NZ, 2023 Aus, 2025 SA). A player only gets credited if the official squad list actually names them — being a contemporary, being a big name, or "probably would have been picked" does not count (mirrors the existing precedent already applied correctly elsewhere in this file, e.g. Jason Gillespie/Brett Lee excluded despite being squad-adjacent because they were injury-replaced before the tournament). This is now mandatory for every new player added and every existing player whose data gets touched for any reason — but per the user's explicit Day 32 decision, this is NOT a mandate to retroactively re-audit all 275 existing players; the Gavaskar case was fixed as a one-off, and this rule exists to stop the mistake from recurring going forward, not to trigger a full backlog project.
- `pickTodayPlayer()` in wordle.js only selects the daily mystery player from players that have `bowlingStyle` defined (i.e., have been through this verification pass) — this expands automatically as each country batch gets done, so nobody is ever shown as the day's answer with unverified data.

FORMAT RULE — THIS IS CRITICAL:
- The `format` field must show the EXACT combination of official international formats a player has played — never a lossy catch-all. There are 7 valid values: `Test`, `ODI`, `T20` (single format only), `Test+ODI`, `Test+T20`, `ODI+T20` (exactly two formats), and `All-format` (all three).
- "Playing" a format means at least 1 official international match: a Test, an ODI, or a T20I.
- IPL, Big Bash, domestic T20, franchise cricket, ICL — none of these count as T20I (or any international format). Only bilateral/ICC-event international caps count.
- If a player retired before T20 Internationals existed (before Feb 2005), they cannot have a T20I credit — full stop, no need to even check.
- **PERMANENT FIX (Day 26, session 3):** the field used to be a lossy binary — "All-format" (all 3) vs "ODI" (used as a catch-all for "not all 3", regardless of which 2 formats, or even just 1, a player actually played). A user-flagged bug (Yuzvendra Chahal showing "ODI" despite having played ODI+T20I) triggered a full audit of every player marked "ODI" — 95 players were reclassified using the 7-value scheme above. The audit also caught 2 genuine pre-existing errors unrelated to the scheme change: Chaminda Vaas and Inzamam-ul-Haq had each actually played 1 T20I (confirmed via ESPNcricinfo/Wikipedia) and are now correctly `All-format`, not `Test+ODI`. It also caught 2 players who had zero ODI caps despite being labelled "ODI" (Devdutt Padikkal, Dhruv Jurel — both actually `Test+T20`), and 6 players with zero ODI *and* zero T20I caps who were mislabelled "ODI" instead of `Test` (Manav Suthar, Anshul Kamboj, Akash Deep, Sarfaraz Khan, Shahbaz Nadeem, KS Bharat). `wordle.js`'s `comparePlayer()` was rewritten to compare formats by set-overlap (green = identical combination, yellow = share at least one format without matching exactly, grey = no shared format) instead of a hardcoded "All-format is special" rule — this generalizes correctly to any of the 7 values.
- **One player (Harsh Dubey) stayed flagged rather than resolved:** he received a T20I squad call-up for India's July 2026 Zimbabwe tour, but an actual match cap could not be confirmed at time of writing — kept as `ODI` with a comment flag rather than asserted as `ODI+T20`, same treatment as other unconfirmed-attribute cases (see KL Rahul precedent below).

VERIFIED FORMAT REFERENCE EXAMPLES:
- Brian Lara → Test+ODI (retired 2007, zero T20Is — Southern Rocks T20 in 2010 was domestic Zimbabwe cricket)
- Shane Warne → Test+ODI (retired January 2007, zero T20Is — IPL was franchise cricket not T20I)
- Wasim Akram → Test+ODI (retired 2003, T20I format did not exist)
- Kapil Dev → Test+ODI (retired 1994, T20I format did not exist)
- Sourav Ganguly → Test+ODI (zero T20 Internationals for India — all T20 appearances were IPL/domestic only)
- Rahul Dravid → All-format (played 1 T20I vs England in 2011 — verified on ESPNcricinfo and Britannica)
- Yuzvendra Chahal → ODI+T20 (ODI debut and T20I debut both June 2016, zero Test caps)
- Devdutt Padikkal → Test+T20 (Test debut 2024, T20I debut 2021, explicitly confirmed zero ODI caps)

BEFORE ADDING ANY NEW PLAYER TO players.js:
- Always verify all 3 formats on ESPNcricinfo first — check the Debut/Last Matches page for the Test, ODI, AND T20I sections individually. Do not stop checking once you find one or two — confirm the presence or explicit absence of all three before picking the format value.
- Never assume a player is All-format (or any particular combination) because they played IPL, Big Bash, or other domestic/franchise T20 cricket.
- Always add a comment line with the verification source and match counts per format.

## WORDLE PLAYER CAP-NUMBER EXPANSION PROJECT — PERMANENT (Added Day 24)

Started Day 24 after the Cricket Wordle roster (130 players, all 11 major Test nations) sat unchanged since the Day 16 verification overhaul. Goal: grow the roster systematically rather than ad hoc, using each country's official Test/ODI/T20I cap-number sequence as the source list.

**ROUND 1 — POPULAR PLAYERS PASS (Added Day 25):** before running the cap-number method (below) on any country/format cell that hasn't started yet, first add the **top 15 all-time leading run-scorers** for that country in that format, plus the **top 15 all-time leading wicket-takers** for that country in that format (30 names total, per format, before dedup). Reason: this taps well-known, popular players first — the kind users are actually likely to guess in Wordle — rather than starting from obscure recent cap-number debutants. Dedup rule is the same as the cap-number method: skip anyone already in the database (from the original 130, or from an earlier expansion batch); an all-rounder appearing on both the batting and bowling top-15 list only counts once. Once this Round 1 pass is done for a cell: if it already produced 20+ genuinely new players, that format is done (same "slightly over target is fine" precedent as India Test finishing 21/20). If it produced fewer than 20 new players (heavy overlap with existing database), move on to Round 2 (the cap-number method below) to fill the remainder up to 20. **This rule applies starting with India, not Australia** — India's existing 20/20 (Test, ODI, T20I) additions, all done via the cap-number method alone before this rule existed, count as India's Round 2 for each format. India's Round 1 (top 15 run-scorers + top 15 wicket-takers per format) has NOT been done yet and is now owed — the next India session should run that pass across all 3 formats, adding any of those popular players who aren't already in the 191-player database as additional entries on top of the 20/20 already reached (same "over target is fine" precedent as India Test already sitting at 21/20). Same cross-check standard applies to Round 1 players as everything else (Four-Source Verification Rule, dedicated IPL-history search per player).

**ROUND 2 — CAP-NUMBER METHOD (the original method, used to fill whatever Round 1 didn't cover):**
- For each country, work through formats in this order: **Test → ODI → T20I**.
- For each format, find the country's current highest cap number (i.e. the most recently debuted player in that format) as of the session date.
- Work backward from that cap number, adding players to `players.js` (full 8-attribute profile: country, role, battingStyle, bowlingStyle, debutYear, format, iplTeams, iccTrophies) until **20 genuinely new players** have been added for that format, counting Round 1 additions toward the same 20. (Changed from an original target of 50 to 20 on Day 24, session 4 — same mechanism, smaller target.)
- **Dedup rule:** if a cap holder is already in the 130-player base (or added in an earlier expansion batch), skip them — they do NOT count toward the 20. Keep moving to the next-lower cap number until 20 new names are reached. The tracking log below always records the full cap-number range covered, including skips.
- **Cross-check standard is unchanged and non-negotiable:** every player, every attribute — ESPNcricinfo + ICC official records + Wikipedia + Cricbuzz (attempted every time; flag "unreachable" per the standing tool limitation) — same Four-Source Verification Rule as everywhere else in this app. IPL team history always gets its own dedicated search per the Day 15 rule.
- **Supplementary source (added Day 24, session 4):** each country's official cricket board website (e.g. BCCI for India, Cricket Australia, ECB for England, PCB for Pakistan, CSA for South Africa, Sri Lanka Cricket, CWI for West Indies, BCB for Bangladesh, ACB for Afghanistan, Zimbabwe Cricket) may also be checked — particularly useful for exactly the kind of gap that came up in the Day 24 India batch (confirming whether a very recent debutant has actually been capped in ODI/T20I yet, which ESPNcricinfo/Wikipedia search snippets sometimes couldn't confirm cleanly). This supplements, not replaces, the 4 mandatory sources above.
- **Cap-number data-quality caveat (found Day 24):** AI-summarized fetches of Wikipedia's "List of India Test cricketers" gave inconsistent cap numbers near the very end of the table across 3 separate fetches (off-by-one drift), and one fetch invented a player who could not be confirmed anywhere else ("Subham Kumar Bhanja", cap 320) — treated as a hallucination and excluded. Lesson: the exact cap NUMBER assigned to a borderline-latest entry is less reliable than the RELATIVE ORDER of recent debuts (which stayed consistent across fetches) and named facts that can be independently confirmed (e.g. "Anshul Kamboj is cap 318" was confirmed via a direct dedicated search). When in doubt about whether a "latest cap" entry is real, verify that specific name independently before trusting it — don't take a single AI-summarized table read as ground truth for the newest 1-2 rows.
- **NO TARGET, WORK TO THE ACTUAL LIMIT (hardened Day 28):** an earlier version of this rule said "roughly 8-15 fully-verified new players per session" — that was never a target and must not be treated as one. Every session keeps researching and verifying new players for as long as real usable capacity remains, stopping ONLY on a genuine hard limit (context/tokens/turns actually running out) or the assigned scope (e.g. a specific cap-number range) being fully covered — never because a round number was reached. This is heavier research per unit than a quiz question (8 attributes with full cross-checks vs. 1 fact), so expect fewer players per session than questions, but that's a description of the work, not a cap on it.

**PROGRESS TRACKER — update every session, this is how we know where to resume:** for every row below India, "Status" should also note which round is in progress (e.g. "Round 1 in progress", "Round 1 done, starting Round 2", "Done") so a future session knows whether to keep pulling from the top-15 runs/wickets lists or has moved on to the cap-number sequence.

**FIXED TRAVERSAL ORDER FOR AUTOMATION (added Day 40):** now that the scheduled Routine dedicates 2 of its daily slots to Wordle, the next cell to work on each time is the table's own row order below — fixed, NOT self-balancing by pool size or "most interesting," same principle already locked in for the Quiz category rotation. Order: India (done) → Australia (done) → England Test (done) → England ODI → England T20I → New Zealand Test → New Zealand ODI → New Zealand T20I → Pakistan Test → Pakistan ODI → Pakistan T20I → South Africa Test → South Africa ODI → South Africa T20I → Sri Lanka Test → Sri Lanka ODI → Sri Lanka T20I → West Indies Test → West Indies ODI → West Indies T20I → Bangladesh Test → Bangladesh ODI → Bangladesh T20I → Afghanistan Test → Afghanistan ODI → Afghanistan T20I → Zimbabwe Test → Zimbabwe ODI → Zimbabwe T20I. A cell already marked "Done" is skipped to the next one; a cell that's still short of 20 players when reached just continues from wherever it left off (Round 1 then Round 2 as normal) — it does NOT get abandoned partway to move to the next country. The `AUTOMATION LAST WORDLE CELL:` line in Current Build Status records the last cell actually worked, so the next of the 2 daily Wordle runs knows where to pick up without guessing.
