## ON THIS DAY EXPANSION PROJECT — PERMANENT (Added Day 26, session 6)

Started because `onthisday.js` has sat at just 18 events (scattered non-sequentially across the calendar — nothing at all in January, May, August, October, or December) since Day 14. Goal: grow it systematically, one calendar day at a time, the same "keep going until the automation runs out of runway" way the Wordle expansion works.

**SOURCE RULE FOR ON THIS DAY — supersedes the generic Four-Source list for this content area only:**
- ESPNcricinfo + ICC official records + Wikipedia + the featured player/team's own country's official cricket board website (BCCI for India, Cricket Australia, ECB for England, PCB for Pakistan, CSA for South Africa, Sri Lanka Cricket, CWI for West Indies, BCB for Bangladesh, ACB for Afghanistan, Zimbabwe Cricket) — all four must show the exact same date, score, and detail, zero-tolerance AND logic, same as everywhere else in this file.
- For an event centered on one specific country's achievement (a player's record, a team milestone), check that country's board — e.g. Cricket Australia for an Australian batting record, BCCI for an Indian milestone. For a match between two countries, check the board of whichever team's achievement the event is actually about (the record-holder or the winning side), not automatically the host.
- Cricbuzz is still attempted and flagged unreachable every time, per the standing tool limitation noted throughout this file — this doesn't change for On This Day, it's just not one of the 4 sources that must agree.
- Any mismatch across the 4 required sources gets flagged to the user exactly like everywhere else — never guessed, never picked by majority.

**THE PROCESS:**
- Work FORWARD through the calendar day-by-day, starting from **18 July** (the day after this project began) and wrapping around the year (…30 Dec, 31 Dec, 1 Jan, 2 Jan…).
- For each calendar day, first check `onthisday.js` for an existing entry at that exact month+day — if one already exists, skip to the next day (this is what lets a day that already has an event from the original 18 stay untouched rather than getting duplicated).
- For days with no existing entry, research whether any genuinely significant cricket event (any year) happened on that calendar date. Look for up to **3** events per day if that many genuinely notable, well-documented ones exist — never pad to 3 by including a weak/trivial event just to hit a number. A day can end up with 1, 2, 3, or occasionally 0 events after honest research; 0 is fine and expected for some days (the site already falls back to a "Did You Know" quiz fact on any day with no logged event — nothing needs to be forced).
- Every candidate event must clear the source rule above before being added. Same as the Wordle/quiz process: broad discovery search first to find candidates, then dedicated domain-restricted queries per required source to actually verify.
- New entries follow the exact existing schema in `onthisday.js`: `{ month, day, year, title, fact }` — `fact` should include an emoji and match the tone/length of the existing 18 entries.
- **NO TARGET, WORK TO THE ACTUAL LIMIT (hardened Day 28):** an earlier version of this rule said "8-12 new calendar-day entries per automated run" — that was never a target and must not be treated as one. Every run keeps working forward through the calendar for as long as real usable capacity remains, stopping ONLY on a genuine hard limit (context/tokens/turns actually running out) or the assigned day-range being fully covered — never because a round number of days was reached. ("Item" for this content type means a calendar day worked through, not an individual event — a day can yield 0-3 events after honest research.)
- **Resume pointer**: this file's own "Next task" line (or the automation's own log) should always state the next calendar day to research from, so a run picks up exactly where the last one left off without re-scanning the whole year.

**PROGRESS**: as of Day 28 (first automated run under the new time-scheduled system), grown from 18 to 24 events. The 06:00 slot worked the full 18-31 July range in one continuous run (per the new no-target hardcore rule) rather than stopping at its originally-assigned 18-23 sub-range, adding 6 new dated entries: Jul 21 1981 (Botham's Ashes), Jul 22 2010 (Muralitharan's 800th wicket on his last ball), Jul 23 2017 (2017 Women's World Cup final, England beat India by 9 runs), Jul 26 1902 (Australia beat England by 3 runs at Old Trafford), Jul 28 1973 (the first-ever Cricket World Cup — Women's, England won), Jul 31 1956 (Jim Laker's 19 wickets in a Test). The other 25 of the 31 days checked in that window turned up nothing significant enough to add — expected, not an error. Flagged for a future session (not added, out of that run's date range): India Women won their maiden ICC Women's Cricket World Cup on 2 November 2025 (beat South Africa by 52 runs, DY Patil Stadium — Shafali Verma 87, Deepti Sharma 5-for + fifty) — excellent candidate for 2 November once the calendar rotation reaches it (that date is currently open).

**21:00 slot (Day 28 evening) — first run of the new v9 automation workflow (paste-ready output, no direct git push):** the Routine researched a broad 1-29 August sweep and deep-verified 10 candidates, output as a paste-ready block. ## RANKING CHALLENGE EXPANSION PROJECT — PERMANENT (Added Day 27, redesigned same day)

Started because Ranking Challenge (`ranking.js`) had sat at exactly 5 fixed challenges since Day 11 — every session showed the same 5, in the same order, no pool to draw from (unlike Quiz's 330+ question bank or Wordle's 255-player database). Day 27 restructured this twice in one day: first into a simple fixed pool (superseded), then into the growing-leaderboard design below after further discussion with the user about how the game should actually feel.

**HOW THE GAME WORKS NOW:** a play session shows **4 challenges** (changed from 5). Each challenge is a "rank these 5 players by X" question. Two sources feed a session:
- **`RANKING_PARAMETERS`** (in `ranking-challenges.js`) — growing "leaderboard" topics like "Test batting average." Each parameter holds a `leaders` array of players sorted best-to-worst on that stat. At session start, the engine randomly draws a 5-player **window** from that list — see the WINDOW RULE below. Target size per parameter: **25 leaders** (a long-term target, grown gradually over many future sessions — not expected today or this week).
- **`FIXED_CHALLENGES`** — small one-off topics that don't fit a growing sorted leaderboard (IPL title ties, "who debuted earliest," "highest individual score" as a standing record). Stay as a hand-picked set of exactly 5 for now.

**WINDOW RULE — the core new mechanic:** when a parameter's `leaders` list has grown past 5, the game doesn't always show the same top 5 — it randomly picks a 5-player window from anywhere in the list, with one constraint: **consecutive picked players (sorted by their rank in that list) can never be more than 3 ranks apart.** This keeps every generated challenge a plausible, tightly-clustered comparison (e.g. ranks 6, 8, 9, 11, 14 is fine — gaps of 2,1,2,3; ranks 1, 2, 3, 20, 21 is not, gap of 17 is too big) instead of jumping from an obvious #1 to an obscure #20. Because the leaders list is already sorted, the correct answer for any drawn window is just that window's own order — nothing needs to be hand-written per instance. Implemented as `pickWindowIndices()` in `ranking.js` (deterministic construction, not retry-based — an earlier retry-based version had a real bug where it could randomly fail ~10-20% of the time on a freshly-started 5-player list and silently drop that topic from the session; fixed same day, verified with 2,200+ trials at list sizes 5/12/25 with zero failures).

**STALENESS RULE — permanent, applies to every leader added to any parameter:** for career-cumulative stats (Batting Average, Most Runs, Strike Rate, Most Centuries, Bowling Average, Most Wickets, Economy Rate, Most Five-Wicket Hauls, Most Maiden Overs, all Fielding/WK stats, Most Matches Played, Most Wins as Captain, etc.) — only fully retired players may be used. An active player's number keeps moving, which could silently flip a ranking's correct order without anyone noticing — worse than a single stale quiz fact, since a ranking's correctness depends on the relative order of all 5. One-off record stats (Highest Individual Score, Best Bowling Figures, Most Catches in an Innings/Match) may use an active current record-holder, same as the Question Quality Rule's "growing record" handling elsewhere — flag it as a still-standing record in a comment.

**IF A CELL CAN'T FIND ENOUGH RETIRED QUALIFIERS:** T20I and IPL skew toward active players since both are younger/shorter-career formats. If a cell can't find enough clearly-retired players who meaningfully qualify (a reasonable minimum threshold, e.g. min innings/wickets/matches so the list isn't skewed by tiny sample sizes), don't stretch to include an active player in a cumulative-stat parameter. Either use a smaller qualifying pool if still meaningfully accurate, skip the cell for now and flag it, or reframe as a dated/closed record instead.

**THE FULL TOPIC TAXONOMY (locked in Day 27):** four top-level buckets, each with sub-parameters, each sub-parameter targeted across all 4 formats (Test / ODI / T20I / IPL) where the stat sensibly applies:

- **BATTING** (8 sub-parameters): Batting Average, Most Runs, Strike Rate, Most Centuries, Highest Individual Score (one-off record), Most Fifties, Most Sixes, Most Fours
- **BOWLING** (7 sub-parameters): Bowling Average, Most Wickets, Economy Rate, Best Bowling Figures (one-off record), Most Five-Wicket Hauls, Bowling Strike Rate, Most Maiden Overs
- **FIELDING** (incl. wicket-keeping) (7 sub-parameters): Most Catches (fielder, non-keeper), Most Dismissals (wicketkeeper — catches + stumpings), Most Stumpings, Most Catches (wicketkeeper only), Most Run-outs Effected, Most Catches in a Single Innings (one-off record), Most Catches in a Single Match (one-off record)
- **OTHERS** (7 sub-parameters): Earliest Debut, Most Trophies/Titles Won, Most Matches Played, Most Wins as Captain, Most Player-of-the-Match Awards, Most Player-of-the-Series/Tournament Awards, Youngest Debutant

That's 29 sub-parameters. Not every sub-parameter × format combination will turn out to make sense (e.g. maiden overs are vanishingly rare in T20I/IPL) — rather than pre-deciding every exclusion now, a cell that turns out non-sensical or unresearchable gets skipped and flagged when a session actually reaches it, same handling as the "can't find enough retired qualifiers" case above. Realistic total once non-sensical combinations are excluded: well under the theoretical 29×4=116 ceiling, but still a large, multi-month project on the scale of the Wordle cap-expansion work.

**BUILD PLAN (revised Day 28, supersedes the original two-phase plan below):** the two-phase plan (5-player starter, then grow later) is retired. New rule: **every cell now gets filled to 25 players in one go** when it's worked on — no more separate "breadth pass" and "depth pass." Before any brand-new cell is started under this rule, the 7 cells that were already sitting at 5 under the old plan must first be expanded to 25 each, in this order: `test-battingavg` (✅ done, see below), `test-bowlingavg`, `test-runs`, `test-wickets`, `odi-runs`, `odi-wickets`, `odi-battingavg`. Once all 7 are done, resume the normal Phase-A-style rotation (category → sub-parameter → format) but filling each new cell straight to 25 instead of 5.

**AVERAGE-TYPE CELLS (battingAvg, bowlingAvg) — literal real record list, decided Day 28:** these cells use the actual unfiltered highest/lowest-average order (min ~20 innings), same standard as Runs/Wickets cells, NOT curated for fan recognition. This means some technically-higher-average but less-famous names outrank household names — e.g. Test Batting Average's real top 25 includes Adam Voges, Graeme Pollock, George Headley, Herbert Sutcliffe etc. ahead of players like Kohli, whose real Test average (46.85) doesn't crack the top 40 and was dropped from that cell for that reason (Runs/Wickets/other cells are unaffected — he still appears there if he qualifies under the staleness rule). "Popular players first, literal exact order later" like the Wordle Round 1/Round 2 split does NOT apply here — average cells go straight to the literal list.

**Progress as of Day 28:** `test-battingavg` expanded from 5 → 25 (literal real order, Four-Source verified: Bradman, Pollock, Headley, Sutcliffe, Barrington, Weekes, Hammond, Sobers, Sangakkara, Hobbs, Walcott, Hutton, Kallis, Chappell, Tendulkar, Lara, Miandad, Dravid, Mohammad Yousuf, Younis Khan, Ponting, Hussey, Chanderpaul, Gavaskar, Steve Waugh). `test-bowlingavg` expanded from 5 → 25 (min 200 wickets, literal real order: Marshall, Garner, Ambrose, Trueman, McGrath, Donald, Hadlee, Philander, Muralitharan, Imran Khan, Steyn, Lindwall, Pollock, Waqar Younis, Wasim Akram, Holding, Lillee, Ashwin, Grimmett, Fanie de Villiers, Walsh, Statham, Bedser, Warne, Anderson) — Ravichandran Ashwin's small mismatch (ESPNcricinfo 24.00 vs Wikipedia 23.95) was resolved by the user (Day 28): keeping 24.00. New cell `odi-battingavg` added at 5 (Bevan, de Villiers, Dhoni, Amla, Richards) — still needs expanding to 25 under the new rule. `test-runs` expanded from 5 → 25 on Day 30 (literal real order, retired-only: Tendulkar through Matthew Hayden — see Day 30 log for full list and the two self-resolved mismatches, Michael Clarke and Inzamam-ul-Haq). `test-wickets` expanded from 5 → 25 on Day 31 (literal real order, retired-only: Muralitharan through Chaminda Vaas — see Day 31 log for the full list, the 5 active-player exclusions, and the tied pairs at 362/355). Remaining backlog: `odi-runs`, `odi-wickets`, `odi-battingavg` (all still at 5, all need → 25).

**SOURCING METHOD:** same Four-Source Verification Rule as everywhere else (ESPNcricinfo + Wikipedia + ICC official records + Cricbuzz-attempted-unreachable, zero-tolerance AND logic, mismatches flagged to the user rather than resolved by guessing). Query efficiently by asking for the whole group of players' stat per source rather than one query per player — though for exact per-player averages, direct Wikipedia infobox fetches proved more reliable than grouped search synthesis (see Day 28 session log).

**BATCH SIZE:** since cells now go straight to 25, expect roughly 1 cell fully completed per session (25 verified leaders is comparable total research to a full Wordle cap-number batch).

## DAILY SESSION FORMAT

Every session must follow this structure:
1. START: "Last session we completed [X]. Today we are building [Y]."
2. BUILD: Make the changes directly in the code, one feature at a time.
3. TEST: Tell me exactly how to test that the feature works.
4. DEPLOY: Commit to GitHub and deploy to Vercel automatically.
5. END: "Today we completed [X]. Next session we will build [Y]."

  Day 17 (session 4) — Merged the question-bank architecture per the user's request: Daily Quiz and Category Quiz used to be two separate files (`questions.js` for Daily, `category-questions.js` for IPL/Test/ODI/T20/Poetry) that had grown 14 genuine duplicate questions between them — the same fact tested twice under different phrasing (e.g. Lara's 400*, Kumble's 10/74, RCB's IPL 2025 win, Dravid's "Wall" nickname). Built one new central file, `question-bank.js` — a single 72-question array, each question tagged `category: ipl | test | odi | t20 | general` (`general` = format-agnostic trivia like LBW/golden duck with no category-quiz tab of its own). Daily Quiz and Speed Round now draw randomly from the whole tagged bank; Category Quiz filters by tag. Poetry got consolidated into its own single repository too, per the user's explicit instruction: the 12-question Poetry Quiz moved out of `category-questions.js` into `poems.js` as a new `poetryQuizQuestions` array, living alongside the existing 6 poems. `questions.js` and `category-questions.js` are both deleted. Updated `category-quiz-v2.js` to filter `questionBank` by category (or read `poetryQuizQuestions` for the poetry tab), and `app.js`'s home-page live stat counter to read the new structure. (2) Discovered and documented two structural, permanent facts about IPL content specifically: ICC official records don't cover the IPL at all (it's a BCCI domestic league, not an ICC event — not "unreachable" like Cricbuzz, genuinely inapplicable), and there is no real official "IPL MVP" trophy — nearly every "MVP" claim online is actually ESPNcricinfo's own unofficial "Smart Stats" analytical ranking, not a BCCI-awarded prize, and it's inconsistently reported. Per user decision: for IPL facts specifically, the 3rd/4th source slots become ESPNcricinfo + Wikipedia + iplt20.com (official IPL site) with Cricbuzz still flagged unreachable every time; MVP is dropped as a question category entirely (not a verifiable official award). One process note: for the Kumble fact, ICC's official site had no article on this specific bilateral-series incident (not a source disagreement, just no coverage — same pattern as the standing IPL/ICC gap) — verified instead on ESPNcricinfo + Wikipedia, which agreed in full detail; Cricbuzz unreachable as usual across all 9 moments, flagged every time.   Day 26 (session 4) — Real bug fix, user-flagged: the Cricket Wordle Format clue showed "ODI" for Yuzvendra Chahal despite him having played both ODI and T20I (just no Test) — because the `format` field only ever had 4 possible values (`All-format`, `Test`, `ODI`, `T20`), and `ODI` had been used as a lossy catch-all for "not All-format" since Day 24, regardless of which formats a player actually played. Per the user's decision, redesigned the field to show the exact combination played: 7 possible values now — `Test`, `ODI`, `T20` (single format), `Test+ODI`, `Test+T20`, `ODI+T20` (exactly two), `All-format` (all three). Delegated the mechanical part (reading each of the 95 players currently marked "ODI" and determining their true formats from their own existing verification comments — no new research needed for most) to a subagent, then personally resolved 9 cases the subagent flagged as unclear with live web searches. That resolution surfaced 2 genuine pre-existing data errors, unrelated to the scheme redesign itself: **Chaminda Vaas and Inzamam-ul-Haq had each actually played 1 T20I** (confirmed via ESPNcricinfo Statsguru and Wikipedia/ESPNcricinfo respectively) and were wrongly labelled "ODI" ("very few/no T20Is") when they should have been `All-format` all along. It also surfaced 2 players with zero ODI caps mislabelled "ODI" instead of `Test+T20` (Devdutt Padikkal, Dhruv Jurel), and 6 players with zero ODI *and* zero T20I caps mislabelled "ODI" instead of plain `Test` (Manav Suthar, Anshul Kamboj, Akash Deep, Sarfaraz Khan, Shahbaz Nadeem, KS Bharat) — all caught by actually reading their own comments closely rather than trusting the field value. One case (Harsh Dubey) stayed genuinely unresolved despite 3 separate searches — he has a T20I squad call-up for India's July 2026 Zimbabwe tour but an actual match cap couldn't be confirmed — kept as `ODI` with a comment flag rather than asserted. Applied the bulk of the reclassification (87 straightforward value swaps: 55 → `Test+ODI`, 6 → `Test`, 26 → `ODI+T20`) via a PowerShell script, since Node/Python aren't available in this environment's shell, and handled the 5 special cases (Vaas, Inzamam, Padikkal, Jurel, Dubey) as individual hand-edits with updated comments. Rewrote `wordle.js`'s `comparePlayer()` format logic from a hardcoded "All-format is special" rule to genuine set-overlap comparison (green = identical combination, yellow = share at least one format without matching exactly, grey = no shared format) — this generalizes correctly to all 7 values instead of needing special-casing. Updated the rules screen text in `wordle.html` to describe the new system instead of the old binary "ODI / All-format" framing. Added a permanent rule to the FORMAT RULE section above documenting the 7-value scheme and this whole fix. Caught and fixed one process slip along the way: the PowerShell script's default UTF8 encoding silently added a UTF-8 BOM to the start of `players.js` that wasn't there before (confirmed via `git show HEAD:players.js`) — stripped it back out before finalizing. Verified via static checks (the project's Browser-pane tool was temporarily unavailable for the rest of this session — the classifier it depends on was down): players.js and wordle.js both parse with zero syntax errors (checked via a separate JS engine, cscript's JScript interpreter, since Node/Python aren't available here), brace count is balanced at exactly 243 open/243 close matching the player count, the new format-value counts (135 All-format + 55 Test+ODI + 26 ODI+T20 + 11 Test + 10 T20 + 4 ODI + 2 Test+T20) sum to exactly 243, and all 5 hand-edited entries read back correctly from the file.   Day 27 — Ranking Challenge rebuilt from a fixed 5-challenge list into a growing, tagged pool — the architecture piece needed before Ranking could be automated the same way Wordle/Quiz/On This Day were. **Architecture change:** split challenge data out of `ranking.js` into a new `ranking-challenges.js` pool file (`RANKING_CHALLENGES` array, same split pattern as `question-bank.js`/`players.js`), each entry tagged with `statType` (`battingAvg`/`bowlingAvg`/`runs`/`wickets`/`highestScore`/`debutYear`/`titles`) and `format` (`test`/`odi`/`t20`/`ipl`/`general`). `ranking.js`'s engine now randomly draws 5 distinct challenges from the pool per session (`pickRandomChallenges()`) instead of always showing the same fixed 5 in the same order. Also generalized the old hardcoded "MI/CSK IPL-titles tie" special case into a reusable `tiedGroups` field (`scoreChallenge()`/`isPositionCorrect()` helpers) — any future challenge can mark players as tied without needing custom code. **New permanent staleness rule** (see RANKING CHALLENGE EXPANSION PROJECT in CLAUDE.md): career-cumulative stats (battingAvg/bowlingAvg/runs/wickets) may only use fully retired players, since an active player's number moving over time could silently flip a ranking's correct order — stricter than the Question Quality Rule's usual "growing record" handling because a ranking's correctness depends on relative order across all 5, not just one number. **First new batch (4 challenges) added**, all Four-Source verified (ESPNcricinfo + Wikipedia + ICC official records + Cricbuzz-attempted-unreachable, zero mismatches): Test bowling average (Malcolm Marshall 20.94/376 wkts, Joel Garner 20.97/259, Curtly Ambrose 20.99/405 — confirmed as the only three 200+ wicket bowlers under a 21 average — Glenn McGrath 21.64/563, Dale Steyn 22.95/439); ODI most runs (Tendulkar 18,426, Sangakkara 14,234, Ponting 13,704, Jayasuriya 13,430, Jayawardene 12,650 — Virat Kohli, currently 2nd all-time and still active in ODIs, deliberately excluded per the new staleness rule); ODI most wickets (Muralitharan 534, Wasim Akram 502, Waqar Younis 416, Vaas 400, Pollock 393 — one ESPNcricinfo search snippet briefly returned "392" for Pollock, resolved via a clean Wikipedia infobox cross-check to 393, not a genuine source disagreement, same self-resolution pattern used elsewhere in this project); Test most runs (Tendulkar 15,921, Ponting 13,378, Kallis 13,289, Dravid 13,288 — matches the figure already verified in this project on Day 17 — Cook 12,472 — Joe Root, currently 2nd all-time and still active, deliberately excluded). Pool grew from 5 to 9 challenges. Wrote the new permanent **RANKING CHALLENGE EXPANSION PROJECT** section in CLAUDE.md defining the fixed statType/format rotation grid (16 cells; Test row now fully complete, ODI battingAvg is next), the staleness rule, sourcing method, and batch size (3-5/run). Verified live in the browser: structural integrity check passed on all 9 pool challenges (zero issues), confirmed `pickRandomChallenges()` draws 5 distinct challenges with no repeats across 20 trials, confirmed tie-aware scoring works correctly both when the generalized `tiedGroups` logic should award full marks (MI/CSK swapped) and when it correctly should not (a non-tied team placed in a tied slot), and played a full 5-challenge session end-to-end (25/25, 500 XP, finish screen rendered correctly) with zero console errors.
  Day 27 (session 2) — Redesigned Ranking Challenge a second time the same day, after the user clarified what they actually wanted: not a fixed pool of pre-built 5-player challenges, but a growing per-topic leaderboard that the game randomly samples from every session. **Architecture rebuild:** `ranking-challenges.js` now holds two sources — `RANKING_PARAMETERS` (growing topics like "Test batting average," each with a `leaders` array sorted best-to-worst) and `FIXED_CHALLENGES` (small one-off topics that don't fit a growing sorted list: IPL title ties, earliest Test debut, highest ODI score). `ranking.js` gained `generateChallengeFromParameter()` + `pickWindowIndices()`: every session, each parameter's challenge is generated on the fly by drawing a random 5-player **window** from its leaders list, with the rule that consecutive drawn players can never be more than 3 ranks apart — keeping every generated comparison a plausible, tightly-clustered one instead of jumping from an obvious #1 to an obscure #20. Since the leaders list is already sorted, the correct order is just the window's own order — nothing hand-written per instance. `pickWindowIndices()` uses a deterministic span-distribution construction (not retry-based), verified with 1,200+ trials across list sizes 5/12/25 with zero failures. Session size dropped from 5 to 4 challenges per the user's explicit choice. **Full topic taxonomy locked in** (see the rewritten RANKING CHALLENGE EXPANSION PROJECT section above): 4 top-level categories — Batting (8 sub-parameters), Bowling (7), Fielding incl. wicket-keeping (7), Others (7) — each sub-parameter targeted across Test/ODI/T20I/IPL where it sensibly applies, roughly 29 sub-parameters total. Two-phase plan: Phase A gets every sensible cell to a 5-player starter list (current phase); Phase B grows each toward the 25-player target over many future sessions, same pace as the Wordle cap-number project. Today's 6 existing growing-parameter topics (Test battingAvg/bowlingAvg/runs/wickets, ODI runs/wickets) were tagged with the new `category` field (batting/bowling/others) to fit the taxonomy — left at 5 leaders each per the user's explicit sequencing ("first fix parameters, then increase to 25 for each"), no new research forced today. Verified live: window-selection mechanism tested directly (n=5 always returns the full list in the one valid order; n=12 and n=25 mocks produced hundreds of unique, all-valid windows with zero constraint violations), full 4-challenge session played end-to-end with no console errors, "Challenge 1 of 4" and the updated finish-screen text render correctly.

  **Automation infrastructure problem found and diagnosed:** the scheduled Cloud Routine had been silently losing all its work — at least 2 separate runs each researched and Four-Source verified a batch of general-knowledge questions (11-12 each), committed locally, then hit a hard, persistent 403 on every `git push` attempt (tested across multiple branches). A GitHub API-based fallback push was also attempted but abandoned as too risky for a ~139KB file. Diagnosed the root cause via a long joint troubleshooting session with the user across claude.ai and github.com: checked Installed GitHub Apps (only Vercel), Authorized GitHub Apps (an unrelated, unused, identity-only "Claude" grant), Authorized OAuth Apps (only Git Credential Manager, which is what lets *interactive* sessions push — using the user's own local machine credentials, unrelated to the cloud Routine), and both types of Personal Access Tokens (none exist). The Routine's own Connectors tab shows `Claude_Code_Remote` attached with an explicit "write access enabled" claim in the UI — contradicting the actual 403s. Concluded this is a platform-side bug/limitation on Anthropic's end, not fixable from account settings; user was advised to report it to Anthropic. **Practical workaround agreed and implemented:** rewrote the Routine's entire Instructions prompt (v9) so it never attempts any git write command at all — it still clones/pulls (read access works fine), does the full research/verification/structural-validation work, then ends every run by printing a structured, copy-pasteable output block (new entries in ready-to-paste JS, recommended CLAUDE.md tracker updates, verification notes, flagged-for-review items) for the user to paste into an interactive session for actual application and push. The old AUTOMATED RUN LOG / PENDING REVIEW / TODAY'S SUMMARY CLAUDE.md-writing mechanism was removed from the prompt since the Routine can no longer persist those sections itself — that bookkeeping now lives in each run's output block instead. **Real bug found and fixed during this session's live verification:** submitting a Wordle guess via a real click on "Submit Guess" (rather than pressing Enter or clicking an autocomplete suggestion) silently failed to register whenever the autocomplete dropdown was still open. Root cause confirmed via `elementFromPoint()`: `.autocomplete-list` in `wordle.html` was `position: absolute` with `z-index: 100`, so it visually floated on top of the Submit Guess button below it (nothing reserved space for it), and the click landed on the dropdown item instead of the button underneath — a real, pre-existing latent bug (not something this session's data change caused), likely present since the autocomplete feature was first built on Day 10. This would affect any player who types a full name by hand and clicks Submit directly, without first clicking the suggestion or pressing Enter. Fixed by removing the `position: absolute; top: 100%; left: 0; right: 0;` and `z-index: 100` rules from `.autocomplete-list` — it's now a normal in-flow block element that pushes the Submit button down while open (matching the input's exact width) instead of covering it, and returns to its original position once hidden. Verified live: confirmed via `elementFromPoint()` that the Submit button is now the actual top element even with the dropdown open and showing a match, then completed two full real-click guess submissions (Mitchell Marsh, then Josh Inglis) with the dropdown open each time — both rendered correctly, attempts counter decremented correctly each time, no console errors.

Since Firebase's own scheduled Cloud Functions require the paid Blaze plan (a card on file), built the sender as a **free GitHub Actions cron job** instead (`automation/send-notifications.js` + `.github/workflows/send-notifications.yml`) using a Firebase service-account key stored as an encrypted GitHub Secret (`FIREBASE_SERVICE_ACCOUNT`) — never committed to the repo, added `.gitignore` patterns as a safety net against ever accidentally committing that file. Client side: `notifications.js` (new, shared) handles requesting browser permission, saving the FCM token to the user's Firestore doc, and a foreground toast; `firebase-messaging-sw.js` (new, site root) handles background notifications; a "🔔 Push Notifications" toggle card was added to profile.html. **Real design correction mid-build:** the user initially wanted notifications to not be optional ("make it a feature the user must receive"), which isn't technically possible — every browser requires an explicit user-granted permission popup before any site can send a notification, no exceptions, and this doesn't change based on Android/iOS version for websites specifically (unlike native apps, where pre-Android-13 apps could get away without asking — explained this distinction to the user in detail since they'd observed it on their phone). What we could do instead: move the ask from a passive Profile-page toggle to a proactive one-time popup shown right after a user's first-ever completed quiz (`maybeShowFirstQuizNotifPrompt()` in notifications.js, wired into quiz.js's save-success callback, gated on `quizzesPlayed` being 0 before that save) — the moment they're most likely to say yes. Verified live throughout: profile.html and quiz.html both load with zero console errors; the notification card renders correctly in all 3 real states (Enable/Disable/Blocked-by-browser); the first-quiz prompt correctly stays hidden when permission is already decided and correctly renders with the right buttons when it should, tested by temporarily mocking `getNotificationPermissionState()` and `firebase.auth()` rather than writing to production Firestore; confirmed Firestore correctly rejects a write from a fake unauthenticated test UID (proving security rules are properly locked down, not a bug). Committed and pushed across 2 commits (`bb09b4b`, `43cb72d`). **Still needs a real end-to-end test** (tracked as the next task): user needs to (1) enable notifications on the real live site with their own account, and (2) manually trigger the GitHub Actions workflow (`workflow_dispatch`) to confirm a real send actually reaches a device.

Cheapest realistic path if/when this gets picked up: wrap the existing website via Capacitor or a Trusted Web Activity rather than rewriting in React Native/Flutter — reuses everything already built. **User's decision: defer, don't build now** — noted here as a real future item, not started, no cost committed yet. - Day: 39 — Built the PWA (Progressive Web App) — the item the user asked to fold into Phase 2 right after the Day 36 native-app discussion, to be finished before moving on to Phase 3. Added `manifest.json` (name/icons/theme colour/standalone display), 3 app icons (`icon-192.png`, `icon-512.png`, `icon-maskable-512.png`, plus `icon-180.png` for Apple's touch-icon convention) drawn programmatically via HTML canvas in the browser — no image-generation or conversion tool (ImageMagick, etc.) was available in this environment's shell, so the icons were rendered directly on a canvas element (dark navy background matching the site's `#0a0e1a`, gold gradient badge matching the `#f4a900`→`#e07b00` brand gradient, 🏏 emoji centered) and the resulting PNG bytes extracted and decoded to disk via PowerShell — genuine, valid PNGs, not placeholders (verified by checking each file's PNG header and IEND footer bytes directly). One real process catch during this: the first icon-180 extraction attempt was hand-transcribed from a large inline tool response and silently truncated (decoded to only 8.5KB instead of the expected ~37KB) — caught before committing by checking the decoded byte count looked implausibly small, and fixed by forcing the browser tool's file-save path (padding the return value to exceed the inline-response size limit) instead of ever hand-copying a large base64 string again. Extended the existing `firebase-messaging-sw.js` (rather than adding a second, competing service worker) with install/activate/fetch handlers implementing network-first caching with an offline fallback — deliberately not a precache-everything strategy, so users online still always see the freshest content (matching how often this project pushes new quiz questions/players/events) while offline visitors get a safety net. Added a `pwa.js` (service worker registration + a custom install-prompt button, since capturing `beforeinstallprompt` and calling `preventDefault()` lets the site show its own button instead of relying on the browser's default mini-infobar) to all 12 HTML pages, plus `<link rel="manifest">`, `theme-color`, and `apple-touch-icon` tags in every page's `<head>`. Verified live: manifest.json fetches and parses correctly with all fields intact, all 4 icons return 200 with valid PNG bytes, the service worker registers successfully at scope `/`, the custom install button renders correctly when a (simulated) `beforeinstallprompt` event fires, zero console errors across the full page-load/registration flow. Committed and pushed (`438473d`). Updated the App Distribution Strategy section's STATUS line to reflect this. **This completes the "PWA now" half of the Day 36 app-distribution decision — Play Store/App Store remains parked, no cost committed, per that section.**

Generated all 4 app icons (badge-only crop, no wordmark — standard practice, icons are too small for text to read) and a separate navbar logo (badge + full wordmark) via HTML canvas, same PNG-extraction method proven on Day 39. **Two real mistakes, both caught by the user via screenshot, not by me:** (1) the first navbar crop excluded the tagline entirely (an unrequested judgment call that the tagline "wouldn't be legible at small sizes" — user corrected this, they wanted the complete logo, not an edited-down version); (2) the second attempt still cut off the bottom of the "CRICTAKKAR" banner before its actual edge, because a color-threshold heuristic trying to auto-detect the boundary was fooled by the image's own decorative circuit-line background pattern and produced a false "gap" reading. - Day: 42 — **Phase 3 started.** Two threads, both in this session.
  **(1) The multiplayer hosting plan turned out to be dead, and was replaced.** The tech stack section has always said "Socket.io + Node.js on Railway.app (free tier)". Verified live this session that Railway's genuine free tier no longer exists — it is now a one-time $5 trial credit followed by $1/month of credits, against a minimal always-on service costing roughly $0.80–1.00/month, so it permanently scrapes the ceiling with no headroom. Render's free tier does not support WebSockets at all (paid plans only), and Vercel/Netlify cannot host WebSockets either (serverless functions only). Presented the user with two honest options and they chose **Option A (Firestore-only, no server)**, explicitly asking that **Option B (a real Socket.io server) be written down as a documented upgrade path to revisit after all of Phase 3 is built** rather than discarded. Both are now permanently recorded in the new **PHASE 3 MULTIPLAYER ARCHITECTURE** section above, including the data model, the free-tier quota math that forces its exact shape (a naive one-doc-per-player design costs ~100,000 reads for a single 100-player match and blows the daily quota in one game; the chosen "host aggregates into one leaderboard field" design costs ~2,000 reads / ~1,010 writes for the same match), the three accepted limitations of Option A, and the four trigger conditions that should make us revisit Option B. Worth remembering from that write-up: **Option B's strongest argument is anti-cheat, not latency** — a server is the only way to stop the client reporting its own score, which becomes non-optional the moment a sponsored prize contest ships. Committed as `0723e57`.
  **(2) Built the first working multiplayer feature** — private rooms (#29) + Classic Mode (#31) + live leaderboard (#35), in `multiplayer.html` / `multiplayer.css` / `multiplayer.js`. Create a room, get a 6-character code, share it on WhatsApp, and play a synced 10-question quiz with standings between every question. The host's browser drives all phase transitions (answering → reveal → standings → next) and is the only client that reads the answers subcollection; everyone else listens to a single match document. Scoring was deliberately extracted into a pure `computeLeaderboard()` with no Firestore or DOM coupling — partly so it is testable without a database, and partly because it is exactly the function that moves onto the server if Option B is ever taken. Home page "Start Battle" and "Challenge a Friend" now route here instead of the coming-soon popup. Also added `firestore.rules` to the repo for the first time, which **resolved audit item S2** (see the SCALE & SAFETY AUDIT section — it had flagged this exact file as untracked). Committed and pushed as `324f353`.
  **Verified live in the browser:** JS parses with all functions defined; 20,000 generated room codes were all 6 characters, contained zero ambiguous characters (no O/0/I/1/L), and produced zero collisions; question selection returns 10 unique valid indexes with no duplicate across 500 runs; lobby renders correctly as both host and guest with correct badges, join-order sorting and player counts; **a username containing `<img src=x onerror=...>` renders as plain text and does not execute** (no element created, handler never fires); layout holds at both desktop and 375px mobile with no horizontal scroll, long usernames truncating without pushing the HOST badge off the row, and the room code optically centred; the question screen renders the right question/options/fact straight from `question-bank.js`, the reveal marks the correct answer and the player's own wrong pick while leaving untouched options clean, and the countdown ticks 15→14→13 correctly. `computeLeaderboard()` was unit-tested across six scenarios including score carry-forward between questions, wrong answers scoring zero, players who never answered keeping their existing score, a player who leaves mid-match dropping off the table, and a stray answer from a non-player being ignored — all correct. End-to-end click from the home page through to the multiplayer login gate confirmed working.
  **(3) The live site failed on first use, and the fix was two-part.** The user deployed, hit "Create a Room" on `cric-takkar.vercel.app`, and got an alert saying to check their connection. That message was wrong and hid the real cause. Root cause, confirmed by having the user paste their actual Firebase console rules: **the console had no `/matches` rule at all** (only `users` and `communityPoems`, last edited 15 Jul 2026), so Firestore's default-deny blocked every multiplayer request. Fixes, in commit `29412e9`:
  - **The room code is now the document ID rather than a field.** The original lookup used a two-filter query (`roomCode == X` AND `status in [...]`), which requires a composite index in Firestore and was a genuine second candidate for the same symptom. Making the code the ID removed that variable entirely and is better anyway: finding a room is now a direct `get()` instead of a query, joining costs one read, and collision safety comes free because `create` only succeeds on a document that doesn't already exist. Only one `.where()` remains in the whole file (single-field equality on the answers subcollection, which uses an automatic index).
  - **`explainFirebaseError()` replaces the generic alert** and names the real problem per Firebase error code — `permission-denied` now says the rules probably aren't published rather than blaming the player's internet. Worth keeping in mind as a general lesson: a catch-all "check your connection" message cost a full debugging round trip here.
  **(4) Merged the repo rules with the live console rules — which surfaced a real pre-existing bug** (commit `5549d30`). Diffing against what the user actually had live turned up three things beyond the missing `matches` block, all now fixed in `firestore.rules`:
  - **Deleting a community poem was silently broken whenever the poem had comments.** `poetry.html:293` batch-deletes a poem's comments along with the poem, but the live comment rules granted delete permission to nobody, so the whole batch failed. The merged rules allow a comment to be deleted by its own author *or* by the author of the poem it sits under (the second case is the batch-delete path, since those comments belong to other people).
  - **Any signed-in user could delete any other user's poem** (`allow update, delete: if request.auth != null`). Now author-only.
  - **Poem updates could rewrite `authorUid`/`authorName`.** Now pinned, while still allowing likes, reports and `commentCount` writes from other users, which the app genuinely needs.
  Every existing write path (poem create/like/report/delete, comment create, the `commentCount` transaction, profile writes) was checked against the new rules before handing them over. `users` delete is now blocked — nothing in the app performs one.
  **✅ The user published the merged rules on Day 42.** S2 is therefore fully closed: the file is both committed and in force. Note for future sessions: those rules still contain `allow read: if signedIn()` on `/users/{userId}`, which is exactly the line audit item **S1** flags as exposing every user's email address to any signed-in account. Publishing did not create that problem — the leaderboard has always needed broad read access on `users` — but it is now codified, and S1 remains the most serious open item in the project.
  **NOT YET TESTED: a real two-player match.** Everything above was verified with mock data and isolated function calls, because testing the live path requires two logged-in accounts and creating accounts / entering passwords is outside what I can do. The Firestore read/write path, the host's phase timing, and cross-device sync have therefore never actually run. This needs a real playtest — open the site on two devices (or two browsers) with two different accounts, create a room on one, join with the code on the other, and play a full match through to the final standings.

Per the standing lesson, every higher-risk claim was independently re-verified rather than trusted — **this caught 2 real errors the automation missed**: (1) **Chris Woakes's IPL team list wrongly included Punjab Kings** — he was bought at the 2024 auction for ₹4.2cr but never appeared in a single match, withdrawing during the season after his father's passing; excluded per the standing "signed but never played" rule (Axar Patel / Stuart Broad precedent), and Wikipedia's own team list confirms no Punjab Kings entry at all. A related false lead was also chased down and dismissed: an iplt20.com search result appeared to place Woakes in a Rajasthan Royals 2026 squad, but his actual 2026 franchise team is Sylhet Titans in the BPL, not an IPL side. - Day: 44 — Built **1v1 Duel (#42)** and **Public Matchmaking (#30)** on top of the existing Battle Friends room engine — both reuse the same lobby/question/scoring code, just with a different entry path onto it. The user asked to work through the parked backlog "in a sequence, one by one"; this was item 1.
  **Data model:** every room now carries `roomType` ('private' | 'duel' | 'public', separate from `mode` which stays free for future Speed/Elimination modes) and `maxPlayers` (10 for private/public, 2 for duel — was previously a hardcoded global constant everywhere).
  **1v1 Duel:** new "⚔️ 1v1 Duel a Friend" button creates a `duel` room capped at 2 players. The moment the 2nd player joins, the host's own lobby render auto-calls `startMatch()` — no manual Start button, since there's nothing left to wait on. Guarded by a `duelAutoStartFired` flag (reset per-room in `listenToMatch()`) so a second Firestore snapshot arriving milliseconds later — the normal optimistic-write-then-server-confirm pattern — can't fire it twice. The final screen also gets duel-specific framing ("You beat Rival!" / "Rival won this one" / "It's a tie!") instead of a rank number, since #1/#2 reads oddly for exactly 2 players; classic/public matches are unaffected and still show rank-based results.
  **Public Matchmaking:** new "🎲 Find a Random Match" button queries `matches` for `isPublic == true && status == 'lobby'`, filters out the player's own room / already-joined rooms / full rooms client-side, and joins whichever qualifying room has the earliest `createdAt`. If none qualify, it creates a new public room and waits, discoverable by the next person who searches. Deliberately avoided combining `orderBy` with the equality filters — that combination needs a manual composite index in Firestore, which would mean sending the user to the Firebase console to click a one-time setup link. Sorting the small candidate list client-side instead needed no rules or index changes at all (multiple plain `==` filters are already covered by Firestore's automatic per-field indexes). Also refactored the old inline join logic in `joinRoom()` out into a standalone `joinRoomByCode()` so both the manual code-entry screen and matchmaking share one code path — matchmaking treats a lost race (someone else joined the same lobby a moment earlier) as a normal case, not an error: it just falls back to starting a fresh public room instead of showing a scary message.
  No `firestore.rules` changes were needed — the existing `allow read: if signedIn()` on `/matches/{matchId}` already covers listing/querying, not just single-document reads, and room creation/joining go through the same host/player-entry rules already in force.
  Verified live (mocked `me`/Firestore calls rather than real accounts, since testing needs no login for structural/logic checks): all new functions and buttons exist with zero console errors; duel lobby with 1 player correctly waits with no Start button; duel lobby with 2 players auto-starts exactly once even across repeated re-renders (confirmed by stubbing `startMatch()` and counting calls); the non-host guest in a duel never sees a Start button; a public lobby's waiting text correctly differs from a private room's; all three duel final-screen outcomes (win/loss/tie) render the correct emoji/title/score line; the classic/private final screen is unaffected (regression check); `findPublicMatch()`'s candidate filtering was verified against 5 mock rooms (own room, full room, already-joined room, and two open rooms at different ages) and correctly picked the earliest one while excluding the other three. **Still not tested: a real two-account matchmaking pairing and a real two-account duel** — same category of gap as the original Battle Friends build before the Day 43 playtest, needs the user to try both with two real accounts. Committed and pushed (`multiplayer.js`, `multiplayer.html`, `multiplayer.css`).

- Day: 45 — Built **Speed Mode (#32)** and **Elimination Mode (#33)**, both on the existing `multiplayer.js` room engine (same reuse pattern as Duel/Matchmaking) via a new `mode` field on the match doc, orthogonal to `roomType`. Two new entry-screen buttons create `roomType:'private'` rooms with `mode:'speed'` or `mode:'elimination'`.
  **Speed Mode**: the clock drops from 10s to 5s (`SPEED_SECONDS_PER_QUESTION`, read everywhere via a new `secondsForMode()` helper instead of the flat constant). Scoring is no longer flat — a new `pointsForAnswer()` uses the `timeTaken` field every answer already carries (that field existed since the original build specifically for this, per an old code comment) on a sliding scale: 20 points for a near-instant correct answer down to a 5-point floor for one at the very last moment, 0 for wrong. Classic Mode's scoring is untouched (still flat 10 per correct) — verified as a regression check.
  **Elimination Mode**: a new pure function `computeEliminations(match, leaderboard, answers, qIndex)` runs inside the host's existing `aggregateAndReveal()` step. Rule: among still-active players, anyone who got that question wrong (or didn't answer) is "at risk"; the lowest cumulative-score player among those at-risk is knocked out — exactly one elimination per question, and if literally everyone active gets it right, nobody goes home that round. The match ends early the moment only one active player remains (a new `countActivePlayers()` check added to the host's "next question or finish" step), rather than always running the full 10 questions. Needs 3+ players to start (`ELIMINATION_MIN_PLAYERS`), enforced in the lobby's Start button same way the existing 2-player minimum was. Eliminated players get a dedicated `eliminatedScreen` ("You're Out! — X of Y still battling…") instead of continuing to see questions — the `render()` dispatcher checks `isEliminated()` before showing the question screen. The final leaderboard for elimination matches is reordered by a new `eliminationOrderedBoard()`: the winner (still active) always ranks first, then eliminated players in reverse elimination order (last one out ranks just below the winner) — this can differ from a pure score sort, which is intentional (a big early lead doesn't matter if you got knocked out in round 4 while someone else survived to round 8 on a smaller score).
  Both modes get a small badge in the lobby ("⚡ Speed Mode — 5 seconds per question…" / "💀 Elimination Mode — lowest score is out…") and a tag on the question screen itself. No `firestore.rules` changes were needed — the host already has unrestricted update rights on the match doc, so writing `mode`/`eliminated` fields needed no new permission.
  Verified live (mocked `me`/match objects via direct function calls, same no-login-needed approach as the Day 44 Duel/Matchmaking build): `pointsForAnswer`/`computeLeaderboard` produced correctly-ordered, correctly-ranged scores for 3 speed-mode answers (instant/near-buzzer/wrong) and left classic-mode scoring flat; `computeEliminations` correctly did nothing when everyone answered right, correctly eliminated the lower-scoring of two wrong answerers rather than the higher one, and correctly refused to eliminate down to zero active players across a multi-round scenario; `eliminationOrderedBoard` correctly ranked an active winner above 3 eliminated players in reverse-elimination order; `secondsForMode()`/`countActivePlayers()`/`isEliminated()` all returned correct values against hand-built match objects; the real DOM was driven end-to-end via `render()` for a 2-player elimination lobby (Start button correctly disabled, "need at least 3 players" note shown), a 3-player elimination lobby (Start enabled), speed/elimination/classic mode badges and question-screen tags all rendering correctly (or correctly hidden for classic), the eliminated-waiting screen rendering the right "Out after Question N" / "X of Y still battling" text, and the final screen rendering the correct elimination-specific title/emoji/subtext for both a winner ("You survived — you won!") and a runner-up ("Eliminated — finished #2 · out after Q4"). Zero console errors throughout. **Still not tested: a real multi-account match of either mode** — same category of gap as every other multiplayer feature before its first real playtest; needs the user to try both with 3+ real accounts (elimination genuinely needs 3, speed just needs 2+). Committed and pushed (`9768bbd`).

- Day: 45 (session 3) — Built **Tournaments (#34)** — knockout brackets (sizes 4/8/16), manual/host-driven for now. User chose the build order directly: knockout format (not round-robin, at least not yet — see below), manual/host-driven first, host-less automation later once this is proven out.
  **The one idea that keeps this simple:** every bracket pairing is just a normal 1v1 duel in the *existing* `/matches` collection — completely unchanged game engine, completely unchanged security rules for matches. The only new mechanism is a **deterministic match ID per pairing** (`"{tournamentCode}-R{round}-S{slot}"`), computed independently by both paired players and the tournament host — nobody ever generates a random code and writes it somewhere for someone else to read. Whichever of the two players' uid sorts first creates that match (so its `hostUid` always equals its real creator, satisfying the existing match-creation rule with zero rule changes); the other player just joins it, exactly like a manual duel — including the existing "2/2 players → auto-start" logic, reused untouched.
  **New collection, `tournaments/{code}`:** `hostUid`, `bracketSize`, `totalRounds`, `players` (lobby map, same shape as matches), `currentRound`, and a `bracket` map keyed `"r{round}_s{slot}"` → `{player1Uid, player2Uid, winnerUid}`. The *entire* bracket shape (every round, not just the first) is written at start time with future rounds' player slots `null` — so the live bracket view shows the whole shape ("Quarterfinal → Semifinal → Final") from the first question, filling in names as rounds resolve, rather than revealing one round at a time.
  **The tournament host's browser is the bracket's authority** — same role a match host already plays for one match, just one level up: it watches the current round's pairing matches (`onSnapshot` on each deterministic match ID) for a `status:'finished'`, reads the winner off `leaderboard[0]`, writes it into the tournament doc, and once every pairing in the round has a winner, computes and writes the next round's pairings (or, if it was the final, crowns the champion). A `advancedPastRound` guard stops a burst of near-simultaneous match completions from writing the same round-advance twice. **Accepted limitation, consistent with the existing one:** the host must keep their tab open for the whole tournament, or the bracket just stalls at whatever round it reached — same category as a match host closing their browser mid-match, now scaled up to the whole event. A `beforeunload` handler marks the tournament `aborted` on a best-effort basis, same as matches.
  **Two real usability gaps were caught and fixed before this could actually work in practice, not left as caveats:** (1) `joinTournamentByCode()` now doubles as **resume** — a player already in the tournament (returning after a refresh, or after finishing their bracket match) is let straight back in with zero writes, even mid-tournament or after it's finished, instead of hitting the old "already started" rejection meant for strangers. (2) The tournament ID is saved to `localStorage` the moment a player is genuinely in one, and read back on page load — so navigating away to actually play a match (`multiplayer.html?matchId=...`) and coming back drops them straight into the live bracket, no code re-entry needed. `multiplayer.js`'s final screen also gained a **"Back to Tournament"** button, shown only when the match carries a `tournamentId`.
  **`multiplayer.js` gained one small, generic, reusable boot addition** (not tournament-specific): a `?matchId=` URL param now skips the entry/join screens entirely — if the visitor isn't yet a player and the match is still in its lobby, it auto-joins them; otherwise it just resumes watching. This one branch is what lets both the pairing's "creator" (already a player, just returning) and its "joiner" (not yet a player) land in the same place with no separate flag needed.
  **`firestore.rules`** gained a `tournaments/{tournamentId}` block that mirrors the matches rules almost exactly (`isTournamentHost()` has unrestricted update rights; anyone else may only add/remove their own entry in the lobby `players` map before it starts) — deliberately no new match-creation permission of any kind, since pairings are created by their own players under the existing rule.
  Scope deliberately left for later, not attempted this session: **Round Robin** (the user said "we will build both," knockout first — round-robin's data model and pairing-schedule logic is a separate, later piece of work), the **host-less/scheduled version** needed for #39's Daily 8pm tournament (same GitHub Actions-as-host approach already sketched in the PHASE 3 ARCHITECTURE section, revisit once this manual version is proven with real users), byes/uneven bracket fills (Start is disabled until the bracket is *exactly* full), and any forfeit/timeout handling for an abandoned pairing (an abandoned match just never finishes, stalling that slot forever — accepted for V1, same class of limitation as everywhere else in Phase 3).
  Verified live (mocked `me`/tournament/match objects via direct function calls — same no-login-needed approach used for every other Phase 3 feature before its first real playtest): all pure bracket-logic functions independently unit-tested — `matchIdForSlot`, `pickCreatorUid`, `roundLabel` (correct across 4/8/16-player brackets: Semifinal/Final, Quarterfinal/Semifinal/Final, Round of 16), `numSlotsInRound`, `buildInitialBracket` (correct full-shape pre-allocation for an 8-player bracket, 7 total slots, round 0 seeded correctly, later rounds correctly null), `nextRoundPairings` and `isRoundComplete` (a simulated 4-winner round-0 completion correctly produced the exact right round-1 pairings). DOM-level: lobby correctly gates the Start button on an *exact* player count and hides it entirely from non-hosts; the live bracket view correctly renders TBD placeholders for undecided future rounds, gold-highlights a recorded winner and strikes through the loser in the same pairing; the "Play Your Match" box correctly appears only for a player in an *undecided* pairing in the *current* round and correctly disappears once their slot has a winner or they're not part of that round at all; a deliberately malicious player name (`<img src=x onerror=...>`) rendered as inert escaped text with the handler never firing, confirming `escapeHtml()` actually protects the `innerHTML` bracket rendering. `maybeAdvanceRound()` was tested against a stubbed Firestore (capturing writes instead of sending them): a completed non-final round correctly wrote exactly the right next-round pairings, a completed final round correctly wrote `status:'finished'` + the right `championUid`, and calling it twice for the same round correctly wrote only once. The champion screen correctly showed the winner's own "You won the tournament!" vs a runner-up's "X won the tournament." On the `multiplayer.js` side: all three deep-link boot branches verified against a stubbed Firestore (match doesn't exist → correctly falls through to the existing "no longer exists" screen; match exists, not yet a player, still lobby → correctly auto-joins; match exists, already a player → correctly resumes without re-joining), and the "Back to Tournament" button correctly shows only when `match.tournamentId` is set. Zero console errors throughout. **Still not tested: a real multi-account tournament** — same category of gap as every other multiplayer feature before its first real playtest; needs the user to try a full 4-player bracket (the smallest size) with real accounts to confirm the actual Firestore read/write path, the deterministic-ID handoff between two real browsers, and the host's round-watching all genuinely work outside of mocked data. **Also needs the user to publish the updated `firestore.rules` in the Firebase console** — same standing lesson as every previous rules change in this project (S2, S1): committing the file changes nothing on its own, tournament creation/joining will fail with a permission-denied error until the new `tournaments` rules are actually live. Committed and pushed (`55619f8`).

- Day: 46 — Built **League + Knockout Playoffs**, a second tournament type in `tournament.js`/`tournament.html`/`tournament.css` alongside the existing Knockout bracket. The user's original ask ("Round Robin") turned out to mean something bigger once discussed: not a single flat round-robin, but a real league — **20 players, split into 2 groups of 10, full round-robin within each group (every player faces every other player in their group once — 9 matches each, 45 per group, 90 total), then the top 4 from each group (8 total) feed straight into the existing knockout bracket engine for Quarterfinal → Semifinal → Final.** Points: Win = 3, Draw = 1, Loss = 0 (the user's answer before the scope correction) — Round Robin duels can genuinely tie, unlike Knockout which forces a winner via tiebreak, so standings needed real draw handling for the first time. Tiebreak for standings: points, then cumulative quiz score across the group, then name.
  **The big win of this design: it needed zero changes to `firestore.rules`.** Every league-specific write (starting the group stage, recording a group result, transitioning into the knockout bracket) is done exclusively by the tournament host, exactly like the existing Knockout rules already allow (`isTournamentHost()` already has unrestricted update rights) — so the whole feature reuses the exact trust model already proven and already live in the Firebase console, not a new one.
  **Round-robin scheduling** uses the standard "circle method" (`generateRoundRobinSchedule()` in tournament.js) — fixes one player, rotates the rest one seat per round, producing 9 rounds of 5 matches with every pair appearing exactly once. Same deterministic-match-ID trick as Knockout (`{code}-G{A|B}-R{round}-P{pairIndex}`), so both paired players independently compute the same ID with no coordination needed.
  **Seeding into the knockout stage** is deterministic, not random — A1-B4, A2-B3, B1-A4, B2-A3 in the Quarterfinal — specifically so the top 2 finishers from the same group can't meet before the Semifinal at the earliest (standard sports-league practice), and because a fixed rule means even if this ever got triggered from two places at once it computes the identical bracket both times, so there's no real race condition to guard against.
  **One real architecture call, different from the group stage's host-authority model in a way worth remembering:** unlike Knockout's `hostWatchCurrentRound` (round-gated — only watches the CURRENT round's matches), the league's `hostWatchAllGroupMatches` watches **all 90 pairings from the start**, since group matches can be played in any order with no round-gating needed. This means the host does need to check back on the tab periodically until the whole group stage wraps up — but explicitly **not continuously**: Firestore's `onSnapshot` delivers the current state of a document the instant a listener attaches, so reopening the tab after being away for a day still catches up correctly on everything that finished while it was closed. The `beforeunload` auto-abort-on-host-leaves handler was deliberately **not** extended to the `'group'` status for this reason (it stayed on `'playing'`/`'knockout'` only) — a 90-match, 20-person group stage realistically spans more than one sitting, so the host's tab closing between check-ins must not blow up the whole tournament the way it correctly does for a small, single-sitting knockout bracket.
  Reused completely unchanged once qualifiers are known: `buildInitialBracket`, `nextRoundPairings`, `isRoundComplete`, `hostWatchCurrentRound`, `maybeAdvanceRound`, `renderBracket`, `buildBracketHTML`, `renderFinal` (with small type-aware label tweaks) — this is the payoff of the original Knockout build already being written as reusable pure functions.
  New UI: a "League + Playoffs" button on the entry screen (needs exactly 20 to start, same "exact fill" pattern as Knockout's bracket-size buttons), and a new `groupStageScreen` with a Group A/Group B tab toggle, a live standings table (rank/player/played/won/drawn/lost/points, top-4 rows gold-highlighted as qualifying), and — only for the viewer's own group — a "Your Matches" list of all 9 opponents with a Play button for each unplayed pairing and a Won/Lost/Draw result line for finished ones. Both the standings table and the matches list are built via `document.createElement`/`textContent` (not `innerHTML` strings), matching the app's existing safe-rendering pattern for user-supplied names.
  Verified live (mocked `me`/tournament objects via direct function calls — same no-login-needed approach as every other Phase 3 feature before its first real playtest, run against a locally-hosted preview since another chat's dev server occupied the usual port): `generateRoundRobinSchedule()` tested at n=10 — confirmed exactly 9 rounds of 5 matches, 45 unique pairs with zero duplicates, every player appearing in exactly 9 matches with zero player appearing twice in the same round; `computeGroupStandings()` tested against a 4-player mock with a mix of wins/losses/a draw — confirmed correct points math and the points-then-score tiebreak correctly separated two players tied on points; `seedKnockoutBracket()` confirmed producing the exact A1-B4/A2-B3/B1-A4/B2-A3 draw, and piping its output through the existing `buildInitialBracket()` confirmed a correctly-shaped 8-player bracket with future rounds correctly left TBD; `isGroupStageComplete()`/`groupTotalPairings()` confirmed correct on partial and fully-recorded mock data; `hostWatchAllGroupMatches()` confirmed it attaches exactly the right number of listeners, is a true no-op on a second call (guard works), correctly skips pairings already recorded on attach, and `clearGroupMatchWatchers()` correctly tears them down; a deliberately malicious opponent/player name (`<img src=x onerror=...>`) rendered as inert escaped text in both the standings table and the "Your Matches" list with the handler never firing; `render()`'s dispatch to the new `'group'` status correctly showed the group screen, hid the lobby, defaulted the tab to the viewer's own group, and populated the matches list only for that group; the lobby's League badge/player-count/start-button-enable logic was confirmed correct at 19/20 (disabled, "Waiting for players…") and 20/20 (enabled) for the host, and correctly hidden for a non-host; the `startTournament()` type-dispatcher was confirmed routing to `startLeagueTournament()` for a league doc and would route to `startKnockoutTournament()` otherwise; zero horizontal overflow at 375px mobile width including with a deliberately very long opponent name (the CSS ellipsis-truncation held); zero console errors across the entire test run. **Still not tested: a real 20-account league end-to-end** — same category of gap as every other multiplayer feature before its first real playtest, and a bigger ask than Knockout's 4-account minimum; realistically this needs to wait until there's a real user base rather than the user's own spare accounts, same reasoning already applied to Speed Mode/Last One Standing. Committed and pushed.
- Day: 47 — Built **Host-Less Scheduled Tournaments (#39 Daily 8pm / #40 IPL season)**: a GitHub Actions bot now plays the tournament-host role with no human browser ever open, running an 8pm Knockout and a 10pm League + Playoffs every single day. Full design, the bye/uneven-split mechanism, and what's verified vs. not are written up permanently in the new **HOST-LESS SCHEDULED TOURNAMENTS** section above — summary here: extracted the pure bracket/round-robin math out of `tournament.js` into a new `tournament-logic.js` (loads in both the browser and Node, so the bot and the UI share one source of truth); added bye-aware bracket building (any signup count, no cap, rounds up to the next power of 2) and flexible group-splitting (any count ≥10, not a fixed 20) as new pure functions; added a `scheduledSignups` Firestore collection (open all day, no cap) plus its rules; built the sign-up UI (two cards on the entry screen with live counts and a Join/Leave toggle, switching to "Enter Live Tournament" once the bot starts it); built `automation/run-scheduled-tournaments.js` + a new 5-minute-cadence GitHub Actions workflow that locks sign-up at start time (cancelling under the minimum — 4 for Knockout, 10 for League), builds the bracket, and polls forward any in-progress bot-hosted tournament by `.get()`/`.update()` instead of a persistent listener; added notifications: a "tournaments are open" reminder 3x daily (9am/3pm/6pm IST), a "starts in 15 minutes" nudge sent to every opted-in user (not just people already signed up — the point is catching last-minute joiners) 15 min before each lock, and a cancellation notice sent directly by the bot to just that night's actual signups. Regression-verified the manual Knockout/League flow is untouched by the refactor, and verified all the new logic — including a full 11-signup Knockout-with-byes run to champion and a full 13-signup League run through group stage into playoffs — by extracting the real functions from both files and running them against a mocked Firestore in the browser (Node isn't available in this environment's shell). **`firestore.rules` published in the Firebase console (Day 47) — the new `scheduledSignups` permissions are live.** Still not done: trigger a real GitHub Actions run (via `workflow_dispatch` first, to confirm the secret/permissions actually work end-to-end in GitHub's cloud rather than just in a mock), and a real signup from an actual account. Model used: Opus, per the Model Selection Rule (new real-time architecture, no human host) — routine follow-up work on this feature can drop back to Sonnet once the architecture is proven live.
- Day: 48 — **Verified the scheduled-tournament bot for real (the Day 47 leftover task) — infrastructure confirmed working.** Manually triggered `run-scheduled-tournaments.yml` via `workflow_dispatch` (the user clicked "Run workflow" on GitHub, since this session's own tools have no logged-in GitHub session and `gh` CLI isn't installed here) — all 8 steps succeeded, including the actual bot script, confirming the `FIREBASE_SERVICE_ACCOUNT` secret and Firebase Admin access genuinely work in GitHub's cloud. Cross-checked the run's log output (`Scheduled-tournament bot run at ... IST` / `Run complete.`, nothing in between) against the bot's own source — confirmed this was the CORRECT behavior (no signups existed yet that run, so zero writes), not a swallowed error. The user then signed up for both tonight's Knockout and League through the live sign-up UI — confirmed via screenshot as a real, successful Firestore write under the published rules (1/4 and 1/10, "You're in — Leave" showing). Both will hit their real 8pm/10pm IST lock time tonight via the actual cron schedule (not a manual trigger) with only 1 signup each, so they should auto-cancel with a real push notification — full confirmation of that (and of a real signup from an actual account, the other Day 47 leftover) is deferred to the next session's start, once tonight's window has passed.
  **Built Host Migration & Graceful Leaving** (see the new permanent section above), fixing the exact gap the user described in their own words: *"if the host leaves the tournament should continue without breaking — just the person who left leaves, not all — and same applies to non-host as well."* Clarified scope with the user first (the tournament no-show case — a bracket pairing where a player never shows up to play at all — needed a separate explanation with a concrete example before the user could decide), and the user's explicit call: **wait 4 minutes, then give a walkover.**
  Investigated first rather than assuming: found a non-host leaving mid-MATCH already didn't break anything (the match runs on wall-clock schedule regardless of who answers) — the two real gaps were (a) the HOST of a match or tournament vanishing (previously killed the whole thing outright, or worse, silently froze it forever on a crash with no clean tab-close to trigger even that), and (b) a tournament bracket pairing whose own player never shows up at all, permanently blocking that round.
  Built a shared presence-heartbeat + `effectiveHostUid()` mechanism (every player checks in every 20s while something's actually in progress; the recorded host stays host while fresh, otherwise the lowest-uid still-fresh player takes over — no separate "election" write needed, every client computes the same answer independently) — implemented per-file in `multiplayer.js` (matches) and shared via `tournament-logic.js` (tournaments AND the scheduled bot, so a bot-hosted tournament gets the exact same protection a browser-hosted one does). Rewrote `firestore.rules` to allow any current player to write their own presence/left-status, or to claim an abandoned `hostUid` with a narrowly-scoped single-field write — a deliberate, small trust extension consistent with this app's existing no-money-at-stake posture (Scale & Safety Audit S3). Rebuilt `multiplayer.js`'s match clock from a client-local chain of 3 `setTimeout`s (unrecoverable if that client vanished) to a single anchor-timestamp-based step re-evaluated on every snapshot, so a migrated host picks up mid-phase with the correct remaining time instead of restarting or losing the clock. `leaveRoom()`/`leaveTournament()`/`beforeunload` now write a graceful `leftPlayers` flag instead of aborting everything. Added `computeWalkovers()` (4-minute-since-round-started, exactly one side present) to `tournament-logic.js`, wired into both `tournament.js`'s live bracket view and the bot's `advanceKnockoutTournament()` — deliberately scoped to round-gated Knockout/playoff rounds only, NOT League's group stage (which is designed to span hours/days at each player's own pace, where a 4-minute clock wouldn't mean the same thing).
  Verified extensively via the browser (no Node available locally, no logged-in test accounts either) — every new pure function tested directly against real staleness/migration/walkover scenarios (17 pass across `effectiveHostUid`/`isPresenceFresh` in both files, 8 pass on `computeWalkovers` including the "too early," "both present," "both absent," and "byes/decided slots never touched" cases), `driveHostPhase`'s remaining-time math tested with a stubbed `setTimeout` across all 3 phases including an overdue-clamps-to-zero case and a missing-`phaseAt` fallback, `maybeActAsHost`/`maybeActAsTournamentHost`/`claimTournamentHostIfNeeded` tested end-to-end against a stubbed Firestore including a full walkover-completes-a-round-and-advances cycle, and the leaderboard's new "left" tag checked directly in the DOM (including an XSS-safety check with a malicious player name). **NOT yet verified: any of this against a real crash/disconnect across two or more real accounts** — needs the updated `firestore.rules` published in the Firebase console first (same standing lesson as every previous rules change in this project), then a real test where one tab is deliberately closed mid-match/mid-tournament to confirm another one actually takes over.
